An email address longer than 254 is no address and fails with `FCMS2108` first. `FieldLengths` in core holds the defaults for the storage model, the validator and slug derivation. A single `select` is sized by its maximum length or 64, never by its options, so an added option never needs a wider column, which schema sync would only report; schema validation refuses an option longer than that with `FCMS1211`, and a field with longer options declares a `maxLength` (#2879). A multiple field, blocks and a repeated component that declare no maximum hold at most `FluentCms:Content:MaxItems` items (1,000), refused with `FCMS2103` as a declared maximum is, so that a call of the SDK or an import, which no request body bounds, cannot store a list of any length; a declared maximum, higher or lower, holds instead ([#3307](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3307)).

**Indexes.** Primary and unique keys above; `[Indexed]` fields; `[Unique]` fields as a partial unique index `WHERE state = 1`, with `AND "{column}" IS NOT NULL` when the column is nullable, scoped by table with the draft side checked in the validation pipeline: `(site_id, locale, field)` on `cms_{type}_i18n` for localized fields, `(site_id, field)` on `cms_{type}` for non-localized fields (that table has no `locale`), and the same without `site_id` for `ContentScope.Global` types, whose `site_id` is null and would defeat the index; GIN indexes on `jsonb` columns on PostgreSQL when `[Indexed]` is set on a JSON field.

`[Indexed]` on a column field is a plain index `ix_{table}_{column}`, named after the table that holds the column:

- `(site_id, state, field)` on `cms_{type}` for a non-localized field.
- `(site_id, locale, state, field)` on `cms_{type}_i18n` for a localized field, because lists always filter on one locale.
- The same without `site_id` for `ContentScope.Global` types.
- Nothing for a relation or media field, because `cms_reference` already indexes the target. On a JSON field it is the provider's JSON index above.

Key indexes are named `uq_{table}_...`, which leaves `ux_{table}_{column}` for `[Unique]` fields and `ix_{table}_{column}` for `[Indexed]` fields. A column flattened from a single component is named by its prefixed column, and `[Indexed]` and `[Unique]` on the component's field index that column.

**Identifier budget, as built.** PostgreSQL keeps the first 63 bytes of an identifier and drops the rest without an error, SQL Server allows 128 and SQLite has no limit, so 63 is the budget on every provider. Schema validation holds the names that are stored or mapped to it (`FCMS1218`, naming the type and the field): a type name and a field name have at most 64 characters, the size of `cms_document.type`, `cms_reference.source_type`, `field` and `target_type` and `cms_activity.target_type`; a content type's tables at most 63, so a collection or singleton name has at most 59 characters, or 54 when it is localized; a column, flattened from components included, at most 63; and the path of a relation or media field, which is its name in `cms_reference.field`, at most 64. Index and check names are built from the table and the column, so a valid schema may still make them longer, and they are shortened instead of refused. A name over 63 characters keeps its first 54, without a trailing underscore, and ends in `_` and the first 8 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the whole name: `ux_cms_customer_testimonial_highlight_i18n_hero_section_configuration_background_image_alt_text` becomes `ux_cms_customer_testimonial_highlight_i18n_hero_sectio_358eeeb1`. This is how EF shortens the names it makes, with a hash in place of EF's counter, so the result does not depend on the order of the model, and two names with the same first 63 characters, which PostgreSQL would cut to one and then refuse as a duplicate, stay apart. It covers the names core gives (`uq_`, `ux_`, `ix_`) and those a provider convention gives (`ck_..._json`, `gin_`), after the conventions run. A name that fits is unchanged, so no existing database sees a rename. Names are ASCII, so a character is a byte. This answers [#2873](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2873) of the review of 7 Nov 2026 ([#2847](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2847)): the budget is PostgreSQL's 63 bytes.

**Text, NULL and dates across providers, as built.** Three behaviours that differed on SQL Server now match the other providers:

- A `[Unique]` index over a nullable column (see "Nullability") is filtered on `state = 1 AND "{column}" IS NOT NULL` on every provider. SQL Server holds NULLs equal in a unique index, and EF adds its own `IS NOT NULL` only to an index with no filter, so two published entries with an empty `[Unique] email` collided there alone. SQLite and PostgreSQL never compare NULLs in a unique index, so for them only the definition changes. The validation pipeline already skips an empty value.
- On SQL Server, the text columns of content fields that are not JSON have the binary collation `Latin1_General_100_BIN2`, the one the JSON dialect applies to JSON text, through `IStorageConventions.ConfigureTextColumn`. Equality, `contains`, `startsWith`, the `>` and `<` with which a cursor continues a sort, and `[Unique]` are then case-sensitive, as on SQLite and PostgreSQL (text has no `lt` or `gt` filter), so `Hello` and `hello` slugs no longer collide on SQL Server alone, and text sorts by its UTF-16 units, which is code point order except around U+FFFF (see "Text order across providers" below). System columns keep the database's collation. `startsWith` keeps its `IndexOf == 0` check, because SQLite's `LIKE` ignores case.
- `SqlServerStorageConventions` writes every `DateTimeOffset` in UTC, system columns included, as `PostgreSqlStorageConventions` does, so `createdAt` reads back with offset zero on every provider instead of in the writer's offset. The converter keeps the column type, so the system migrations do not change.

A host's next content migration on PostgreSQL or SQL Server rebuilds the unique indexes of nullable columns with the new filter, and on SQL Server changes the collation of the content text columns. Schema sync on SQLite compares only whether an index is partial, so it reports nothing. These behaviours are checked on the model and on the SQL that EF generates (`OfflineProviderTests`), and the filter and unique tests run on SQL Server too ([#2711](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2711)). This answers [#2874](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2874) of the review of 7 Nov 2026 ([#2847](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2847)): a binary collation on content text columns, and NULL left out of a nullable `[Unique]` index on every provider.

**Text order on PostgreSQL, as built ([#3308](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3308)).** On PostgreSQL the same text columns have the collation `C` through `ConfigureTextColumn`, so a sort, and the `>` and `<` with which a cursor continues it, follow the code points, as on SQLite and, but for the characters above U+FFFF, under SQL Server's binary collation, and no longer the database's collation, which is a language's on most installs (`en_US.utf8` in the official image) and put `apple` before `Banana` and `_cherry` beside `cherry`. Equality, `contains`, `startsWith` and `[Unique]` do not change, since a database's default collation is deterministic. JSON columns and system columns keep the database's collation, as on SQL Server: no content query sorts or compares a system text column by range, so the system and plugin baselines do not change and a database made by `1.0.0-preview.1` is not refused for this. `ContainsIgnoringCase`, `ILIKE` on PostgreSQL, serves only the member list's `display_name`, a system column; on a `C` column it would fold ASCII letters alone. Nothing changes a content table that exists at start. The content text columns are the `text` and `character varying(n)` columns of `cms_{type}` and `cms_{type}_i18n` other than `locale`: a `slug`, `url`, `email` or `select` field and a text field with a maximum length are `character varying(n)` (`FieldLengths`), the rest `text`. Changing one keeps its type, `ALTER TABLE cms_article ALTER COLUMN slug TYPE character varying(200) COLLATE "C"`; PostgreSQL rewrites no rows for it, since the type stays, but rebuilds each index on the column and holds an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock on the table until it is done, so reads and writes of the type wait, which is work for a maintenance window. A host with content migrations runs `fluentcms migrations add` with a name of its own as soon as it upgrades: with no schema change the migration holds only an `AlterColumn` to `C` for each content text column, and `fluentcms migrate` applies it. Left alone, the alters wait for the host's next content migration and go in with an unrelated change. Schema apply does not change a column's collation, so for a host without content migrations a table made before keeps the database's collation on its columns, and the plan lists each such column under `Left alone:` ([#3476](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3476), "Schema apply as built" below). A column that schema apply adds, or whose type or nullability it changes, gets `C`, since `ContentSchemaDiff.Alter` takes the model's collation; a column it only renames keeps the one it had, and is listed. Such a database is recreated, with its content exported and imported, or its columns are altered by hand with the statements that the query in the notes of `1.0.0-preview.2` writes, each with the column's own type: `TYPE text` on a `character varying(n)` column would leave a type change that the next schema apply plans as destructive and refuses without `--allow-destructive`. Checked on the model and the generated SQL (`OfflineProviderTests`), on the migration scaffolded after a snapshot without the collation (`ContentMigrationGeneratorTests`), and by sorting and paging by cursor through titles that differ by case, punctuation and an accent, on each provider (`PaginationTests`).

**Text order across providers, as built ([#3476](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3476)).** A sort on a content text column, and the `>` and `<` with which a cursor continues it, compare under the column's collation, so on each provider a cursor walks every entry once in the order of its sort. The query language has no `lt` or `gt` on text, so the order reaches a client through the sort and the cursor alone. The providers give the same order except around U+FFFF. SQLite's `BINARY` and PostgreSQL's `C`, in a UTF-8 database, compare UTF-8 bytes, which is code point order. SQL Server's `Latin1_General_100_BIN2` compares the UTF-16 units of `nvarchar`, and a binary collation takes no `_SC` flag that would make it read a surrogate pair as one character: a character at U+10000 or above, such as an emoji, is a pair whose first unit is in D800 to DBFF, so it sorts after U+0000 to U+D7FF, as by code point, but before U+E000 to U+FFFF (private use, the CJK compatibility forms, the halfwidth and fullwidth forms, U+FFFD), which by code point it follows. Two texts are ordered differently only where, at the first character in which they differ, one has a character at U+10000 or above and the other one in U+E000 to U+FFFF: `😀` (U+1F600) comes before `Ａ` (U+FF21), and `z😀` before `zＡ`, on SQL Server, and after them elsewhere. For a client: a cursor stays opaque and good only for the database that gave it, and it continues that database's order correctly; a client that sorts, merges or compares pages itself must compare as the provider does, by UTF-16 unit on SQL Server, which is what JavaScript's `<` and .NET's `string.CompareOrdinal` do, and by code point on SQLite and PostgreSQL (by `Rune` or by UTF-8 bytes in .NET); content moved to another provider by export and import can list such texts in another order there. A `varchar(n)` column under `Latin1_General_100_BIN2_UTF8` (SQL Server 2019 and later) would hold UTF-8 and may give code point order, which is yet to be checked on a server, but it changes the type of every content text column, which schema apply plans as destructive and SQL Server refuses on an indexed column, and its `n` counts bytes rather than characters, so it is left to a model change of its own. `PaginationTests.TextAboveUFFFFSortsByCodePointExceptByUtf16UnitOnSqlServer` pins each provider's order in a sort, a descending sort, the pages of a cursor and the page after a cursor that ends on U+FF21.

**Singleton key, as built.** The shared table of a singleton has the unique index `uq_{table}_singleton` on `(site_id, state)`, or on `(state)` for a `ContentScope.Global` singleton, with no filter. An entry has one shared row per state whatever its locales, so the database holds the type to one entry per site. A key on `cms_document` could not: that table has a row per locale, so `(type, site_id)` would refuse a singleton's second locale, and `(type, site_id, locale)` would let two entries in different locales through. The trash keeps an entry's content rows, so a trashed singleton keeps its place until it is purged, which is what the check before the insert already counts (it reads the trash). The index is part of the content model, so it arrives with the host's content migration (`fluentcms migrations add`) or schema sync, like any new index, and needs no system migration. A schema that later turns a singleton into a collection leaves the index in place: schema sync reports the drop and does not apply it, as for any other index.

**The site in every unique key, as built ([#2937](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2937)).** Every unique index of the tables of a `ContentScope.Site` type holds `site_id`: `uq_{table}_document_state` and `uq_{table}_document_locale_state` end in it, and `uq_{table}_singleton` and the `[Unique]` field indexes already began with it. PostgreSQL requires the partition key in every unique constraint of a partitioned table, so with the site in every key now, partitioning the content tables by site later changes no key on an installation. It comes last in the two document keys, because the reads and the purge find an entry's rows by `document_id` alone and still seek them. The tables of a `ContentScope.Global` type keep their keys, since their `site_id` is null. The keys hold the same rows as before: `cms_document` keeps `(document_id, locale)` unique across sites, an entry is read and written in its own site only, and a create takes a new id, so all the rows of an entry carry one site. So the check of a `[Unique]` value before a write is scoped by site as its index is, and the rerun after a unique violation (4.4) meets the same collisions. The primary key stays `id`: a partitioned table would need `(id, site_id)`, which comes with the rebuild that partitioning needs anyway, since PostgreSQL cannot partition an existing table in place. The change is in the content model, so it arrives on every provider with the host's next content migration, which drops and creates the two keys. Startup validation compares tables and columns and passes either way; schema sync on SQLite reports `change index uq_{table}_document_state` for a database made before it and leaves the key as it is. `ContentMigrationGeneratorTests` checks the keys that the SQLite, PostgreSQL and SQL Server migrations create.

**List and trash indexes, as built.** Every content table has `ix_{table}_state` on its scope and `state`, as an `[Indexed]` field index without the field: `(site_id, state)` on `cms_{type}`, `(site_id, locale, state)` on `cms_{type}_i18n`, and the same without `site_id` for `ContentScope.Global` types. `state` is a system column, so no field index takes the name. The shared table of a singleton has none, because `uq_{table}_singleton` covers those columns. Without it a list that filters on no `[Indexed]` field read `cms_document` of every type in id order; with it the list seeks its own rows and sorts them, because SQLite does not take the order on `cms_document.document_id` from an index of the content table. It is part of the content model, so it arrives with the host's content migration or schema sync, like the singleton key. `cms_document` has `ix_cms_document_type_site (type, site_id, deleted_at)`, which the check before a singleton is created seeks (`type` alone for a global singleton), and `ix_cms_document_trash (type, deleted_at) WHERE deleted_at IS NOT NULL`, which the trash purge seeks for one type's trash in the order it was trashed. On `deleted_at` alone SQLite took the first index for the purge and sorted every document of the type. Both come with the system migration `AddDocumentIndexes`. Deleting a site and removing a locale filter `cms_document` on `site_id` and `locale` and still scan it; they are rare administrative writes. `QueryPlanTests` checks these plans on SQLite.

**SQLite connections, as built.** The SQLite provider opens a file database in WAL mode, where readers and the writer do not block each other. A connection interceptor sets `busy_timeout` to 5 seconds on every connection, so a writer waits for the write lock inside SQLite, asks for `journal_mode = WAL` on a connection that may write until SQLite reports it (the mode belongs to the file and persists), and then sets `synchronous = NORMAL`, which in WAL mode loses at most the last commits on a power cut and never the database. A file that cannot change mode keeps the rollback journal and `synchronous = FULL`. EF already creates a new database in WAL mode, so this matters for a file made another way, which the host's first connection that may write switches at start. A connection that SQLite opened read-only (`sqlite3_db_readonly`) gets the busy timeout alone, since leaving the rollback journal writes to the file: the connection that EF's `SqliteDatabaseCreator.Exists()` opens with `Mode=ReadOnly` before anything else, a connection string with `Mode=ReadOnly`, or a file the process may not write. Asking there failed with `SQLite Error 8`, so a host did not start on an existing file in the rollback journal ([#3116](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3116)). An in-memory or temporary database names no file and is left as it is. `cms.UseSqlite(connectionString, writeAheadLog: false)` turns all of it off. `SqliteWriteInterceptor` and `SqliteCommandTransactions` stay in both modes: they run every write in a transaction, which in the rollback journal keeps a writer from starving behind readers. WAL keeps `-wal` and `-shm` files beside the database and needs a local disk ([Docker](../docker.md), [host configuration](../host-configuration.md#sqlite)).

**SQL Server reads, as built ([ADR 0005](../decisions/0005-sql-server-read-committed-snapshot.md)).** A SQL Server database runs with `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` on, so a read at `READ COMMITTED` sees the data committed when its statement started and never waits for a writer, as on PostgreSQL. Under SQL Server's locking default, a read and a write that locked the same rows in different orders deadlocked ([#2959](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2959), [#2963](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2963)). The first step of the SQL Server system migration `Initial` (before B-107 the migration `EnableReadCommittedSnapshot`) turns it on outside a transaction with `ALTER DATABASE CURRENT SET READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON WITH ROLLBACK IMMEDIATE`, which disconnects the other sessions of the database once. It does nothing where the option is on already, as on Azure SQL Database. Where it is off and the user that migrates has no `ALTER` on the database, it fails with a message that names the statement to run. Writes still lock: the job claim keeps `UPDLOCK, READPAST`, a conditional `UPDATE` qualifies rows on the latest committed version, and the schema-history record stays `SERIALIZABLE`.

**JSON dialect.** Filters on block and JSON paths compile to LINQ. `IJsonSqlDialect`, implemented in each data provider package (`jsonb` operators, `JSON_VALUE`, `json_extract`), contributes EF function mappings and LINQ expressions, not SQL text, with one comparison per field type, because a JSON extraction returns the value's own type (on SQLite a JSON number never equals the text `'12'`). The supported set in the first release is: block list contains a block of type *X*, and equality or containment on a scalar path one level inside blocks or JSON, identical on all providers. Deeper paths are deferred and will be PostgreSQL-only when they come (Q7).

**Migration streams.** Three kinds of EF migration contexts share one database, each with its own history table:

| Stream | Owner | Generated by | History table |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| System tables (`cms_document`, `cms_version`, `cms_reference`, identity, jobs, settings, activity, sites, locales, plugins, locks, outbox) | `FluentCMS.Data.{Provider}` | CI, one set per provider | `cms__migrations` |
| Plugin tables (`cms_media_*`, `cms_webhook_*`, ...) | each plugin package, per provider folder | plugin CI | `cms_{plugin}__migrations` |
| Content type tables (`cms_article`, `cms_article_i18n`, ...) | the host project | `fluentcms migrations add <name>` using the host's `SchemaModel` and provider | `cms_content__migrations` |

At runtime one `CmsDbContext` holds the union model for querying, with the system and plugin tables marked `ExcludeFromMigrations`. The migration runner applies the streams in order system, plugins (dependency order), content, at startup when `FluentCms:Database:MigrateOnStartup` is true (default true on SQLite, false otherwise) or through `fluentcms migrate`. `fluentcms migrate --check` lists pending migrations and exits non-zero.

**Runner messages as built.** `FCMS1305` and an `FCMS1301` whose content migration only needs applying end with `run 'fluentcms migrate' to apply the pending migrations, or set FluentCms:Database:MigrateOnStartup to true`, and the schema history logs (9314, 9315) ask to run `fluentcms migrate`, with a start with that option as the alternative, or `fluentcms schema apply` for a host without content migrations ([#3072](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3072)); before B-90 they named the startup option only (#2889). A test parses every quoted `fluentcms ...` in the source with the tool's command tree, so a message never names a command that does not exist.

**The commands of the runner as built (B-90, [#3013](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3013)).** `fluentcms migrate` calls `MigrateAsync`, which applies every stream whatever `MigrateOnStartup` says, refuses a foreign set first (`FCMS1306`) and, for a host with content migrations, records the schema history when no column is missing, and prints what it applied under each stream. What a call applied is what EF names, in the `MigrationApplying` event of its diagnostic listener, for that call's own migrator (`AppliedMigrations`), not what was pending before: two processes that migrate one database at once both find a migration pending, one of them applies it, and the other reports nothing for it. EF's migrations lock is enough on SQLite and SQL Server; Npgsql takes it inside each migration's transaction, after the migrator has read what is applied, so two processes both applied the next migration. `IDataProvider.LockMigrationsAsync`, a member with a default that takes nothing, lets a provider hold the database while the runner checks and applies its streams: `PostgreSqlDataProvider` takes a session advisory lock keyed by `hashtext(current_database() || ':fluentcms:migrations')`, since advisory keys are shared by the server, and releases it explicitly, since a pooled connection keeps its session. The runner takes it before it checks the database against the baselines and reads what is pending ([#3528](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3528)), at start and in `migrate` alike. On SQL Server, the first step of the system baseline, which turns `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` on, disconnects the database's other sessions (ADR 0005), so on a new database whose option is off, a second run that waits for EF's lock meanwhile fails and succeeds when run again; the data tests run with the option on, and `docs/docker.md` says to run one migrate step or let it restart. `migrate --check` lists `GetPendingMigrationsAsync` and exits 1 when anything is pending (`FCMS3303`). `MigrationRunner.ListAsync` gives each stream's applied and pending migrations (`migrations list`), `MigrationsOf` the set of a stream, and `ScriptAsync` the SQL of the host's provider from one migration of a stream to another, through EF's `IMigrator.GenerateScript`, without opening the database; a plugin or content set scaffolded for another provider is refused as `MigrateAsync` refuses it. `migrations script` without `--stream` writes every stream that has migrations in the runner's order, each after a `-- stream {name}` line; `--from` and `--to` name a migration of one stream, so they need `--stream`; an unknown stream or migration is `FCMS3304` or `FCMS3305`. EF writes no idempotent script for SQLite, so `--idempotent` there is `FCMS3306`. A script records no schema history. `migrations remove` (6.1) deletes the newest content migration through EF's scaffolder with a history repository that reports nothing applied and a migrator that refuses any use, because EF's own check opens the factory's connection, and with `--force` reverts the migration in that database; the tool asks the host instead.

**The migrations lock on SQLite as built ([#3177](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3177)).** EF Core 10 takes its migrations lock on SQLite by inserting the row with id 1 and the time into `__EFMigrationsLock` and releases it by deleting the row, so a process killed inside `MigrateAsync` leaves the row, and `AcquireDatabaseLockAsync` then tries again every second with no end, its cancellation token being the only way out; the type is internal and has no option that bounds or clears the lock. Before each stream that has a pending migration, the runner therefore reads `IDataProvider.ReadMigrationsLockAsync`, the row's time on SQLite and null on PostgreSQL and SQL Server, whose locks end with their transaction or session, and waits while it is there, before it logs 9301: it logs once after 30 seconds (9305), then looks every 5 seconds, since EF logs each read, and gives up after `MigrationsLockWait`, 15 minutes, with a `TimeoutException`, at start and in `migrate` alike. The warning and the error name the row and its time, say that the time is when the lock was taken and is never renewed, give the `DELETE` that clears it once no other host and no `fluentcms migrate` runs on the database, and say that a migration that rebuilds a table may have been partly applied, since EF commits around the rebuild and writes the history row last, so that a next run that fails on a table or column that exists needs the backup. On SQLite the read returns null for a database that does not exist yet, so that EF's creator still makes it, in WAL mode. A stream with nothing pending never takes the lock, so a start with nothing to apply is not held up. The row is not cleared on its own: a `fluentcms migrate` beside a host, or another host's start, may be applying a long migration, and only those call `MigrateAsync` (`schema apply` takes `fluentcms:schema-sync`). Two processes that both read no row before either inserts leave the second waiting inside EF, with no warning and no bound, as before.

**The migrations locks of a session as built ([#3192](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3192)).** On SQL Server, EF's `sp_getapplock` on `__EFMigrationsLock` has no lock timeout, so EF's wait for another process's migrations ended at the command timeout, 30 seconds by default, with `Execution Timeout Expired`, which named nothing; on PostgreSQL the advisory lock of `LockMigrationsAsync` waited with no command timeout, without end and without a word. Both now have SQLite's warning and bound. `IDataProvider.TakeMigrationsLockAsync`, a member with a default that takes nothing, takes EF's lock before EF does: `SqlServerDataProvider` opens the stream's context connection, which EF's migrator then keeps open, and runs EF's `sp_getapplock` with `@LockTimeout = -1` and no command timeout; an application lock of the session is counted, so EF's own request in the same session is granted at once, and its release leaves the runner's held until the handle releases every share of the session and closes the connection. A database that does not exist yet is left to EF, which creates it. The runner calls it around its checks and before it reads what each stream has pending (#3528, below), and wraps it and `LockMigrationsAsync` alike: the provider waits until the lock is free or the token is cancelled, and the runner logs 9305 once after 30 seconds and cancels after `MigrationsLockWait`, 15 minutes, with a `TimeoutException` that names the lock (EF's application lock `__EFMigrationsLock`, or the migrations lock of FluentCMS for the lock of the whole run) and says that another process is applying migrations, whose session ends the lock when it stops. A lock granted as the wait gives up is kept. The bound is SQLite's rather than an option of its own: the lock cannot be left behind on these providers, so the bound only ends a wait for a migration that runs longer, which one `fluentcms migrate` before the hosts start avoids. 9305 names the lock in `{Lock}`, which holds the row and its time on SQLite. A timeout of a migration's own commands is unchanged.

**The lock before the checks as built ([#3528](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3528)).** The checks of `FCMS1307` and `FCMS1308` read what a running migration locks, the history table on PostgreSQL and the catalog on SQL Server, so a process that arrived during a migration failed at the command timeout before it reached the wait. A run that migrates, at start or in `migrate`, therefore waits first: it takes `LockMigrationsAsync` before the checks and holds it until its last stream is applied, and takes `TakeMigrationsLockAsync` on the system context around the checks and releases it before it applies anything, since EF takes that lock in the session that migrates and would wait for another session of the same process. It takes it again on each stream's context before it reads what that stream has pending, so that read waits too. A start with nothing pending so waits for a running migration on both servers. A wait that times out before the checks says `this run migrated no stream`, and 9305 then names the system stream. A run that does not migrate (`MigrateOnStartup` false, `doctor`, `migrate --check`, `migrations list`) takes no lock: it must not hold up, or wait 15 minutes for, a process that does. The check of `FCMS1308` reads the catalog once, after the histories of all streams, so that for every caller, with the lock or without, a database that another process is creating is not refused: a stream's tables are committed with or before the row of its history. No share of a lock outlives its handle on a pooled connection: PostgreSQL's handle unlocks, SQL Server's releases until `APPLOCK_MODE` is `NoLock`, since EF leaves a share when it cannot create its history table and releases the runner's when its own acquire fails, each with a timeout of 5 seconds; an acquire that failed or was cancelled, which may have been granted, and a release that failed clear the provider's pool, which ends the session, and so does the handle for a database EF created. The runner logs a failed release (9307, Warning) instead of throwing it, so that the outcome of the migration stands.

**Schema sync and plugin tables as built.** Schema sync builds its context from the options of the host's `CmsDbContext` with the target schema swapped in, so its model maps the plugins' tables as the runtime context does. A table under the prefix `cms_{plugin}_` of a plugin of the plugin host or of a stream registered through `IPluginMigrations` is never a stale content table, whether the plugin maps it, migrates it or neither, and neither is any `__migrations` history table. Nor is one under the prefix of a plugin that the host does not register while the database has its history table `cms_{plugin}__migrations`, as after a plugin is taken out of the host ([#3072](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3072)); the system's `cms__migrations` and the content stream's `cms_content__migrations` name no plugin. A host that runs schema sync with a plugin that owns tables therefore records its schema history row. The code review of 7 Nov 2026 asked whether the plugin-less context was deliberate; it was not (#2872).

**The provider of a migration set as built.** A content or plugin set carries the store types of the provider it was scaffolded for, and startup validation compares names only, so EF would apply a SQLite set to PostgreSQL unnoticed. A provider switch on an existing database is not supported (#2875). Startup and `MigrateAsync` read the provider that EF writes on the model of every snapshot and migration target model: PostgreSQL and SQL Server record their value generation strategy under `Npgsql:` and `SqlServer:`, and SQLite records neither. They compare it with the provider of the context's own model and refuse a set that differs with `FCMS1306`, which names each stream, the set's provider and the host's (`stream content was scaffolded for SQLite; this host runs PostgreSQL, and a migration set applies only to the provider it was scaffolded for`). This happens before anything is applied and without opening the database. The migration files need no marker. A hand-written migration with no target model names no provider and is not refused. The system stream ships with its provider package and is not checked, and schema sync skips the content stream, so a host can sync on SQLite in Development while its content set is for its server provider. A plugin's set is its provider's own (below). Startup still compares names only, although the information schema reader now reports whole store types (#2879).

**Plugin migration sets per provider as built ([#2711](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2711)).** One plugin assembly holds a set per provider, each in a namespace that ends in the provider's set name, `Sqlite`, `PostgreSql` or `SqlServer` (`PluginMigrationSets`), which is the folder `Migrations/{set}` that `dotnet ef migrations add --output-dir` writes. EF finds a context's migrations by the context type, and supports several providers with a migrations assembly or a context type per provider; a plugin has one of each, so each provider's `ConfigurePluginMigrations` calls `UsePluginMigrationSet` with its set name, which replaces EF's `IMigrationsAssembly` for the plugin's context with one that keeps the migrations and the snapshot of that namespace. The host, the foreign-set check and `dotnet ef` all read the migrations through it. An assembly with no set under any of the three names holds one set, as before, which every provider takes and the check refuses on another provider, now naming the missing set (`stream acme was scaffolded for SQLite and has no set in a namespace ending in .PostgreSql`); an assembly with sets per provider but none for the host's provider is refused as `stream acme has no set in a namespace ending in .SqlServer`. The design-time factory passes the startup project's three providers and `dotnet ef`'s arguments to `PluginDbContextDesignTime.Create`, so `-- PostgreSql` after the command scaffolds into the PostgreSQL set. Each set's snapshot is named after it (`PostgreSqlModelSnapshot`), because `dotnet ef` finds an existing snapshot file by name anywhere in the project and would write one set's snapshot over another's. The PostgreSQL and SQL Server sets call their provider's model builder extensions, so a plugin references the two EF provider packages, and `System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs` as the SQL Server provider does, with `PrivateAssets="all"`: its package depends on no provider, and a host loads a set, and the provider it calls, only on that provider. The webhooks, media and sample plugins moved their SQLite sets to `Migrations/Sqlite` and have PostgreSQL and SQL Server sets with the same migrations, media's `MediaTransforms` included; the ids differ by the time each was scaffolded, and the history keeps ids only. On SQL Server the sets use `nvarchar(max)`, as the system migrations do, so one SQL Server set serves SQL Server 2019 and 2025; the plugins keep their JSON in text columns. SQL Server adds `IS NOT NULL` on the nullable column of media's unique folder name index, which leaves root folders to the service's check, as on the other providers. Offline tests check that each set matches the model on its provider and makes the same tables, columns, keys and indexes as the SQLite set, and that the reference host on PostgreSQL or SQL Server passes the check. The data tests do not load the first-party plugins, so CI applies no plugin set to a server; the webhooks and media sets were applied by hand, through the reference host, to PostgreSQL 15 and SQL Server 2025 (ADR 0001).

**The migration baseline as built (B-107, [#3052](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3052)).** Each provider's set of the system stream, and each set of the media, webhooks, MCP, multi-site, AI assist and search streams, is one migration, `Initial`, scaffolded from the model as it was at the end of M7 (10.4, H5). It replaced 20 system migrations on SQLite and PostgreSQL and 21 on SQL Server, and nine plugin migrations for each provider, none of which was published. The snapshots did not change, and the hand-written steps of the old chains that no scaffold writes are carried: on SQL Server the statement that turns `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` on, now the first step of `Initial`, so that a user without `ALTER` on the database stops it before anything is created; the FTS5 table of the search plugin and its three triggers on SQLite and its GIN index on PostgreSQL. The checks, filtered indexes and collations of the old chains came from the model, so the scaffold writes them too. Left out are what the chain did only to upgrade databases with rows: the defaults of `cms_role.require_two_factor` and `cms_webhooks_delivery.manual`, NOT NULL columns it added to tables with rows, which the model does not have and EF never needs, since it writes every column; and the relative raise of the Author role's `permissions_version` when its seeded grants changed, so a new database seeds every role at stamp 1. Startup and `MigrateAsync` refuse a database whose history of the system stream or of a plugin stream holds migrations but not the stream's first, before anything is applied and whatever `MigrateOnStartup` says, and `doctor` names it among the reasons a host would not start, with `FCMS1307` naming each such stream, the first id it applied and the baseline, and saying to recreate the database; previews make no promise to upgrade a database until 1.0.0. The content stream is the host's and is not checked. `BaselineTests` (data tests, on each provider of CI) builds one database with the SQL that `dotnet ef migrations script` wrote for the old chains, which the test project keeps as test data (`Migrations/BeforeBaseline/{provider}/{stream}.sql`, copied to its output and in no shipped assembly), and one with the baselines through `MigrateAsync`, and compares their tables, columns (type, nullability, default, collation), keys, indexes with their filters, checks, foreign keys, triggers, rows and, on SQL Server, `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT`, with the differences above listed and checked to exist. The SQL Server scripts have a `GO` after each column a migration adds, because SQL Server compiles a batch before it runs it and EF runs each command as a batch of its own. Applying `Initial` on SQL Server logs EF's `NonTransactionalMigrationOperationWarning` once, for the step that turns `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` on outside the transaction; that is expected. The sample plugin of the tests and the plugin template already had one migration each; the reference host and the Blog sample have no content migrations, and the template's content folder is empty, so no host chain needed squashing. A model change before 1.0.0 goes into `Initial` of each provider rather than into a migration of its own, since previews do not upgrade a database: `cms_default_grant` (4.8, [#3103](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3103)) was added to the system baseline so, and `BaselineTests` leaves the tables added since the squash out of the comparison after checking that the baseline made them. A database made by an earlier build of a baseline holds that baseline in its history with nothing pending, so EF applies nothing and the first query of a table added since would fail with a raw missing relation: startup, `MigrateAsync` and `doctor` therefore refuse, before anything is applied, a system or plugin stream whose history holds its baseline, that has nothing pending and that lacks a table of its model or a column of one it has, with `FCMS1308` naming each such stream, its baseline and the missing tables and columns (`table.column`), and saying to recreate the database. The columns of `cms_schema_history` are not compared, since a history that cannot be read or written is logged and the start goes on. Indexes of the model are compared by name on the tables the database has ([#3407](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3407)): a missing unique index is refused with `FCMS1308` as `index {name}`, since it would let in rows the model refuses, and any other missing index, as those folded into the baseline after `1.0.0-preview.1` are on a database it made, is logged (9306, Warning) with the stream, its baseline and the indexes, and the host starts. The schema readers list every index that `CREATE INDEX` made, one whose keys are expressions too, with an empty name for such a key, as the search plugin's GIN index on PostgreSQL ([#3530](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3530)); SQL Server indexes no expression, and its reader lists an index on a computed column or with a filter. Schema apply and the schema sync leave alone an index of a content table with an expression key that the content model lacks, since the content model cannot declare one, so one made by hand is neither dropped nor reported (9303); one of plain columns is, as before. The catalog is read after the histories of all streams ([#3528](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3528), above). Adding an index to an existing database would take a migration, which a preview does not have, so such a database is recreated to get it.

**Storage follow-ups as built.** From [#2879](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2879) of the review of 7 Nov 2026 ([#2847](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2847)):

- **Generated keys.** Every Guid id that EF generates goes through `IStorageConventions.ConfigureKey`. The default keeps the v7 generator. SQL Server uses EF's `SequentialGuidValueGenerator`, because `uniqueidentifier` sorts by its last six bytes first, where a v7 value is random, so v7 keys land all over the clustered index. Sequential values rise within a process; across processes they follow the start of each process, not creation. Nothing in .NET orders generated ids by time any more: the rows of an entry, which `EntryStore.DocumentsAsync` sorted in .NET by id, now come from the database ordered by `created_at` and then id. Keyset cursors (query pagination, the trash purge) compare `document_id` in SQL. `document_id` and the other ids that the code sets itself (sites, users, roles, keys, scheduled jobs, activity rows) stay v7 on every provider, so they stay unordered on SQL Server; measuring that belongs to #2711. A value generator is not part of a migration, so no migration changes.
- **Version listing.** `EntryStore.VersionsAsync` selects the metadata columns into `VersionInfo` and leaves `snapshot`, the large column of `cms_version`, in the database.
- **`select` width.** Fixed, as "Default lengths" says.
- **Information schema.** `InformationSchemaReader` also reads `CHARACTER_MAXIMUM_LENGTH`, `NUMERIC_PRECISION` and `NUMERIC_SCALE`, so a column reads as the model's store type: `character varying(200)`, `nvarchar(max)`, `numeric(10,3)`. A size is added to character, binary and decimal types only, because the views give one to integers too. Startup validation still compares names only.
- **Schema sync across nodes.** Schema sync diffs without a lock. When it has something to apply, it takes the `IDistributedLock` named `fluentcms:schema-sync`, waiting up to 15 minutes (a holder that stops loses its lease, and the bound ends a wait on a lease that never runs out by the waiter's clock, with a `TimeoutException` that names the lock and its `cms_lock` row; [#3167](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3167)), and diffs again under it. Nodes that start together then apply each change once, and the second no longer fails on a table the first created. EF's migrations lock belongs to a stream's history, which schema sync does not keep.
- **CLI and version skew.** `fluentcms migrations add` and, since B-90, `migrations remove` load the host beside the tool's own FluentCMS.Core and EF Core; every other project command runs inside the host's own process (6.1), so only these two have the limit. A host built against other versions fails there with a type or member that is missing; the CLI reports it as `{host} could not be used by this fluentcms, which has FluentCMS.Core {tool}; the host has {version}, so use fluentcms {version}`, followed by the cause, instead of a stack trace.

**Providers as built ([#2711](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2711)).** PostgreSQL 15 and SQL Server 2019 and 2025 are supported and tested providers. The data tests run on each of them and on SQLite: `FLUENTCMS_TEST_PROVIDER` names the provider, Testcontainers starts the server once per run, and every test gets a database of its own. CI runs the four `Data tests` jobs, and a failure on any of them fails the run. The runs settled four choices ([ADR 0001](../decisions/0001-storage-spike-outcome.md#decisions-from-the-provider-runs)). PostgreSQL keeps JSON as `jsonb`, which rewrites the text, so reads put each value back in the form a write gives it (`StoredOrder`, where `IStorageConventions.ReordersJson` is true) and rich text compares as JSON. A `DateTimeOffset` is kept to the microsecond on every provider, because `timestamptz` keeps no more: a convention cuts it in front of the provider's converter, and values written with raw SQL take the same cut. A lease has ended once `lease_until <= now`, in the job queue, the outbox, the distributed lock and every claim dialect. SQL Server stores JSON as checked `nvarchar(max)` on every version, as "Column mapping" says.

**Generation rules.** Each schema gets its own EF model, so `IModelCacheKeyFactory` is replaced with one keyed on the schema hash. Migrations are always scaffolded on a fresh context, because a context that has already called `HasPendingModelChanges()` silently emits `CreateTable` for every table. Generated migration files are marked `<auto-generated />`, because EF marks only the designer and snapshot files and the composite index columns in the migration file fail the build with CA1861. On SQLite, `Migrate()` creates `__EFMigrationsLock`, which all streams share; it is the one exception to the `cms_` prefix rule.

**Development loop.** With `FluentCms:Database:SchemaSync = Auto` (default in Development on SQLite only) the content stream is skipped and additive changes (new tables, new nullable columns, new indexes) are applied by diffing the model against the database at startup. Destructive changes are reported, never applied. This gives "schema changes apply without restart" for new fields and keeps production on explicit migrations.

**Development loop outside Development, as built.** A host whose types are data has no compiled content migrations, which is the reference host with a schema file or the sample (Q2). `cms.AllowSchemaSyncOutsideDevelopment()`, in code only so that no configuration key turns it on, makes `SchemaSync` default to `Auto` on SQLite in every environment, with the rules of Development: additive changes apply at start and destructive ones are logged and left undone. `FluentCms:Database:SchemaSync = Off` still turns it off. A provider without schema sync (PostgreSQL, SQL Server) refuses the opt-in when `AddFluentCms` runs (`FCMS3154`), so their content tables stay on migrations. The startup report lists `schema-sync-outside-development` when sync runs outside Development. This is how Q2 works in Production on SQLite. On PostgreSQL and SQL Server a schema file is applied with `fluentcms schema apply` (below), in place of migrations generated from a schema file (10.3, P4).

**Schema apply as built (B-98, [#3021](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3021)).** `fluentcms schema apply` (6.1) is schema sync's engine, run by an operator rather than at start, on every provider. Nothing changes a server database when a host starts unless `MigrateOnStartup` is set (decision 55), and no migration files are made from a schema file: an image cannot load compiled migrations (decision 11), and files beside a schema file would be a second source of truth. `ContentSchemaDiff.PlanAsync` makes schema sync's comparison of the host's model with the database and turns each difference into steps of EF migration operations, which the provider's `IMigrationsSqlGenerator` writes as SQL:

- Additive, applied: a missing table with its indexes, a missing column, and a missing index. A required column with no default added to a table that has rows gets the value a read gives a field that has none, `''` for text and the empty document for rich text, and is refused otherwise (`FCMS3338`). A new column's check constraint is added with it, which SQLite does by rebuilding the table.
- A rename, applied: a missing column whose field lists a former name, or whose component above it does, while the table has a column of that name and the schema does not (`StoredField.FormerColumns`), is renamed and keeps its values. An index named for the old column is then dropped and made again under the new name, and so is the model's check that reads it: on SQL Server, whose reader reports check constraints, the old check is dropped first, and SQLite, which keeps a check's name when it renames the column, makes the check by rebuilding the table. The references that `cms_reference` keeps under a former path of such a field (`StoredField.FormerPaths`), which the schema no longer has, move to its path when the table has rows under it (`rename field article.writer to article.author in table cms_reference`), as a content migration moves them (4.2).
- An index that differs or that the schema no longer has, applied: it is dropped and, if the schema has it, made again. An index holds no data.
- Destructive, refused unless `--allow-destructive` is given (`FCMS3337`): a stale content table dropped, a column the schema no longer has dropped, and a column whose store type or nullability differs altered. On SQL Server the check constraints that read such a column are dropped first and the model's added after.
- Left alone: a table under a plugin's prefix, as schema sync tells one (above), unless it is a table of a type in the newest row of the schema history, which is a content table under any prefix, such as `cms_seo_meta` of a removed type `seo-meta` under `cms_seo_`. A table of a plugin that the host does not register is listed under `Left alone:`, with the plugin's history table, and the script lists it as a comment. Without a history row, or with one that cannot be read, every table under a plugin's prefix is left alone. So is a table whose name has a control character, which no content table has, since a line of its own in the script, such as `GO` on SQL Server, would run.
- Left alone and listed ([#3476](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3476)): a content text column whose collation is not the model's, where the model gives one (`C` on PostgreSQL, `Latin1_General_100_BIN2` on SQL Server; SQLite's gives none) and the column's type and nullability are the model's: `column title on table cms_article, whose collation is the database's default and not C as in the content model; schema apply does not change a collation, ...`. `InformationSchemaReader` reads `COLLATION_NAME` (`DatabaseColumn.Collation`), which PostgreSQL gives as null for the database's default, and the names are compared ignoring case. Such a column is a database made before [#2874](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2874) on SQL Server or before [#3308](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3308) on PostgreSQL, or one altered by hand. Schema apply does not change it, even with `--allow-destructive`: on PostgreSQL the alter rebuilds every index on the column under an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock of the table; SQL Server refuses `ALTER COLUMN ... COLLATE` on a column that an index or a check reads, so the step would have to drop and remake them; and the change moves what every sort and cursor on the column returns, which is the operator's to time. A unique index cannot newly fail on the way to either collation, since both tell apart every two texts that differ, but the rest is reason enough. The operator alters the column by hand, as the notes of `1.0.0-preview.2` show, or recreates the database. A column that schema apply alters for its type or nullability takes the model's collation and is not listed. A PostgreSQL database whose own collation is `C` lists its columns too, since the reader does not resolve the default, whose `datcollate` would misname an ICU default, and an alter to `COLLATE "C"` ends that. With nothing else to do, the run ends `Nothing to apply; what is listed above was left alone, and the schema history records schema {hash}.`, as it does after a table left alone, and exits with 0. The listing is in the plan and the script alone: schema sync runs on SQLite, whose model gives no collation, and startup validation compares only whether tables and columns exist, as it does for a column's type.

Only what brings the content tables up to the schema records the schema history: content migrations, through `fluentcms migrate` or a start that migrates, and for a host without them, schema apply and schema sync ([#3072](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3072)). For such a host `migrate` and a start leave the newest row as it is, and a start logs the drift (9315), which asks to run `fluentcms schema apply`; before, they recorded the host's schema when no column was missing, so the row a run read no longer named a removed type or a field renamed in JSON, and the loss passed without `--allow-destructive`. For such a host schema sync records only when it applied every change and the history counts nothing as lost (below), since it moves no value; for a host with content migrations, which nothing else would record past a loss, it records as before. A new database gets its first row from the first `schema apply`, which records one with nothing to apply too, or from schema sync's first start.

The command also compares the newest row of the schema history with the host's schema (`SchemaSnapshotDiff`), which tells what the tables alone do not, and counts as lost: every change `schema diff` marks destructive (a removed type or field, a changed field type, list or localizable, a changed kind, localization or scope of a type), and a field renamed with `formerNames` whose values are kept in JSON under its former name, a field of a block or of a component kept in JSON, as `migrations add` counts it (4.2); with `--allow-destructive` the columns of such a component's flattened field are renamed all the same. Before [#3059](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3059) a renamed relation and any renamed field of a component counted as lost too. A history whose newest snapshot cannot be read counts as lost too, since renames cannot be checked. A refused run applies nothing. Before it plans, the command refuses a host with content migrations, whose content tables belong to them and whose snapshot a change beside them would leave wrong (`FCMS3335`), and a pending system or plugin migration (`FCMS3336`). A run that applies holds the `IDistributedLock` named `fluentcms:schema-sync`, which schema sync takes too and which it waits for as schema sync does, from the plan to the history row, so that runs started together apply each operation once. EF's migration command executor runs the operations in one transaction, which it commits early only before a command the provider runs outside one, such as the foreign key pragma of a SQLite table rebuild; a database that refuses an operation rolls back the others (`FCMS3339`). The run then adds the row of the schema history as the runner does, failing with `FCMS3340` if it cannot, and a run with nothing to apply adds it too. `--dry-run` prints the plan and applies nothing. `--script` writes the SQL for the database as it is now, in a transaction in the provider's dialect, with the steps as comments, and applies nothing; the next run finds nothing to do and records the history, and after a destructive script it needs `--allow-destructive` too, since the history still says the change loses data. The PostgreSQL and SQL Server readers now return indexes from their catalogs, through queries the providers pass to `InformationSchemaReader`, and SQL Server's its check constraints (`DatabaseTable.CheckConstraints`). Startup validation still compares columns only.

Known gaps: an altered column takes the provider's own conversion, so a change between types that do not convert fails and is rolled back. Where schema apply and a content migration differ for a rename, the outcome is the same: schema apply drops and makes again an index that a migration renames, and it finds the former column and path in the database rather than in the history, so a run with no history renames too.

**Startup validation.** The runner compares the content model with the database and fails with actionable errors (`FCMS1301: table cms_article is missing column subtitle; run 'fluentcms migrations add AddSubtitle'`). For a host with no content migrations at all it names schema apply first: `run 'fluentcms schema apply', or 'fluentcms migrations add AddSubtitle' to give the host content migrations`.

Related decisions: Q5 (property bags, validated by the spike), Q7 (JSON filtering depth).

### 4.4 Content service and query model

Rows 2.3 to 2.8, 2.14, 2.15, 2.19, 1.7, 1.8.

**Service.** `IContentService` works on `ContentDocument` (an ordered dictionary with typed accessors, used by the Management API, MCP and import) and `IContentService<T>` wraps it with a generated or reflection-based mapper. Both expose:

| Operation | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| `GetAsync(id, GetOptions)` | status, locale, fallback, select, populate |
| `QueryAsync(ContentQuery)` | returns `PagedResult<T>` with items, `total` (optional), `nextCursor` |
| `CreateAsync(entry)` | creates the draft; returns the entry with system fields |
| `UpdateAsync(id, entry, expectedVersion)` | full update of the draft: a field left out is cleared, except one the principal cannot read or write, which keeps its value. Conflict throws `VersionConflictException` with the current version. `expectedVersion: 0` adds a locale the entry does not have yet, and needs `update`, not `create` |
| `PatchAsync(id, patch, expectedVersion)` | partial update of the draft with a JSON merge patch; a null removes a field. Its own method, because a class cannot express absence |
| `DeleteAsync(id)` | trash, in every locale |
| `RestoreAsync(id)` | from trash, in every locale |
| `QueryTrashAsync(ContentQuery)`, `PurgeAsync(id)` | list the trash; delete an entry in it for good (4.5) |
| `PublishAsync(id, LocaleWriteOptions?)`, `UnpublishAsync(id, LocaleOptions?)`, `DiscardDraftAsync(id, LocaleWriteOptions?)` | publishing transitions (4.5); publish returns the entry and `warnings` |
| `ScheduleAsync(id, ScheduleRequest, LocaleOptions?)`, `CancelScheduleAsync(id, action, LocaleOptions?)` | per locale |
| `GetVersionsAsync(id, LocaleOptions?)`, `GetVersionAsync(id, number, LocaleOptions?)`, `RestoreVersionAsync(id, number, LocaleWriteOptions?)`, `DiffAsync(id, a, b, LocaleOptions?)` | versions |
| `BulkAsync(BulkRequest)` | create many; update, publish, unpublish, delete by id list or by filter with a required cap; per-item result |
| `CountAsync(CountOptions?)` | the count alone, by filter and status, with the filter, row rule and site of a list and no page or reference read |

**Contract as built.** Every method of `IContentService` takes the type name first. On a save `expectedVersion` is a required `int`, so no caller skips it; on publish, discard and version restore it is optional, in `LocaleWriteOptions`, for a caller that wants to act only on what it reviewed.

- **Refusals.** A principal that lacks the permission on the type gets `ContentAccessDeniedException` (HTTP 401 or 403). An entry that does not exist, is in another site or of another type, or is refused by its row rule gives `EntryNotFoundException` (404), so a client cannot probe; a read returns null. Delete and restore need the operation on every locale of the entry: none reached is not found, some reached is denied. An unreadable type on a read stays the engine's `ValidationException` on `type`, the same as an unknown type.
- **System operations.** An operation with no ambient `OperationContext` throws. `Principal.System` is accepted only from `Origin.Job`, `Origin.Cli` and `Origin.Seed`, entered through `SystemOperation.Begin`; from `Api`, `Admin` or `Mcp` it throws. `Begin` takes the host's `TimeProvider`, which stamps the operation, and an event that is published with no source is stamped by the bus from the operation or from that clock, and `SeedContext.Time` is the host's `TimeProvider` with no fallback, so the SDK reads the system clock nowhere, neither `DateTime(Offset).(Utc)Now` nor `TimeProvider.System` (a test holds it to that) and a host that freezes or offsets its clock is obeyed ([#3310](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3310)).
- **Audit fields.** `created_by`, `updated_by`, `deleted_by` and `author_id` come from `OperationContext.AuditPrincipal` (4.12). Authorization reads `Principal` alone.
- **Row rule and grants after the write.** A create or update that puts the entry outside the row rule that allowed it is refused and rolled back. A field the client sent or a hook changed must be allowed by a grant whose row rule holds for the new entry.
- **Hooks.** A before hook that changes a field outside the principal's grants fails the operation (`FCMS1406`); a change to a `Hidden` or `ReadOnly` field is allowed, since that is what a hook fills.
- **Uniqueness counts the trash**, because the partial unique index does; only a purge frees a value.
- **Bulk.** `BulkAsync(type, BulkRequest)` runs the single call for each entry (`CreateAsync`, `PatchAsync`, `PublishAsync`, `UnpublishAsync`, `DeleteAsync`), each in its own transaction, so every entry gets its own permission check, hooks, validation, publish policy, version, events and activity row, and one that fails rolls back only itself. Its result per entry is `Succeeded`, `Failed` with a `BulkProblem` that holds the code, message and validation errors of the problem the single call would have answered, and the current entry for a version conflict (an entry the principal cannot reach has the same `FCMS3104` as a missing id), `Duplicate` for an id named again, or `NotRun` after cancellation, which is checked between entries. A request by filter needs a cap of at most `BulkRequest.MaxItems` (1000): the filter is read against the drafts, keeps only entries the principal may read and may also perform the operation on, and is read in full before the first write, so one over the cap is refused (`FCMS2207` on `cap`) with nothing written. The operation's row rule joins the read rule in the selection query, on the entries selected and not on their relation targets, so the database returns only entries that can be changed, and a filter that still reaches more than ten times the cap of entries the single check refuses is refused (`FCMS2207` on `filter`). An update by filter applies one merge patch, and a publish by filter publishes, at the version the filter read, so a draft changed since is a conflict; an unpublish or a delete by filter acts on what matched when the filter was read, and both can be undone. A failure that is none of the single call's refusals is logged and reported as `Failed` with `FCMS3199` and a message that names no cause, and the next entry runs. An entry that committed is `Succeeded` even when reading it back fails, which is logged. A value the operation does not use, such as a cap on a request by id, is refused rather than ignored, and a bulk call inside a transaction throws, since there a failed entry could undo the others.
- **One write at a time.** The service is scoped, and the state of the write in progress (its changes, savepoints and commits, which bulk reads to report each entry) belongs to the scope. A hook that calls the service runs in the flow of that write and joins it. A write or a bulk call from another flow while one runs throws `InvalidOperationException` rather than mixing the two, so concurrent operations each need a scope of their own. This answers item 7 of [#2869](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2869).

**Query model.** `ContentQuery { Filter, Sort[], Page, Cursor, Select[], Populate[], Status, Locale, Fallback, Site, RichTextFormat }`. The filter is an AST (`FieldFilter(path, op, value)`, `And`, `Or`, `Not`) with operators `eq ne in nin lt lte gt gte between contains startsWith null notNull`. The same AST is produced by the REST bracket parser, the GraphQL input types, the MCP `query` tool and the typed builder from the source generator.

REST grammar (decision 21):

```text
GET /api/delivery/v1/article
    ?filter[category][eq]=news
    &filter[author.name][contains]=amir
    &filter[or][0][title][startsWith]=A&filter[or][1][title][startsWith]=B
    &filter[sections][hasBlock]=hero
    &q=dotnet                      (needs a search index, 5.7)
    &sort=-publishedOn,title
    &page=2&pageSize=20            (or offset=20&limit=20, or cursor=...)
    &fields=title,slug,author.name
    &populate=author,tags          (depth 1 by default; populate=*&depth=2 up to the cap)
    &status=published              (draft requires a preview key or the draft scope)
    &locale=fr-CA&fallback=none
    &richText=html                 (json | html | markdown)
    &withTotal=false
```

**Compilation.** The AST compiles to LINQ over the property-bag entities. Relation paths join through `cms_reference` to the target type's row in the same state and locale (decision 44: no fallback-aware filtering). Block and JSON paths go through `IJsonSqlDialect`. Row rules from the evaluator are appended as an `And` node before compilation, so authorization and filtering are one query. `state` is inlined as a literal and every user value is bound as a parameter, by reading a member of a holder object, because a bare expression constant becomes a SQL literal. SQLite does not need the inlined `state`, but a literal matches the `WHERE state = 1` index predicate as written, and [#2711](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2711) checks the other providers.

**Population.** Default depth 1, cap configurable (default 3). Beyond the depth a relation is returned as `{ "id": "...", "type": "author" }`. A target the principal cannot read, by type or by row rule, is returned as null at any depth, or left out of a multiple relation. A preview token reads the targets of its entry published, as the principal that minted it reads them (4.7). Cycles stop at the cap. Field selection applies to populated entries with dotted paths.

**Population of other targets, as built (B-67).** A reference whose target is no content type is resolved by the `IReferenceResolver` (SDK, `FluentCMS.Content`) that a plugin registers, scoped, for that target: a plugin field type stored as `Reference` targets its own name, and a rich text `media` node targets `media`. Core knows no target by name; a target named like a content type is that type's, and two resolvers of one target stop the host at start. A read gathers the references it populates at every level, after the relations are filled, and asks each resolver once, in the locale of the read and for the operation, whose principal and surface (`IsDelivery`, 2.2) the resolver may answer by: the media plugin's resolves the public assets of the site's library on the Delivery API, whoever reads, and elsewhere for a principal that may not manage, and by the caller's folder grants for one that may (5.1, [#3109](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3109), [#3112](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3112)). A resolved target may name `CacheTags`, the output cache tags under which its plugin evicts what shows it, and a Delivery read that resolved it carries them (4.10). A reference within the populate depth, named (`populate=hero`, `populate=author.avatar`), under `*` or under a populated relation, becomes the `ResolvedReference.Value` the resolver returns, which holds `id` and `type`, and one it leaves out, because the principal may not read it or it does not exist, is null or leaves its list. A populate path that names a block list or a repeated component (`populate=sections`) resolves the references of other targets inside it at every depth, and `*` does too; relations inside blocks stay references, as before, and a path cannot go further into one (`FCMS2208`). Without populate, beyond the depth, or without a resolver for its target, a reference stays `{ "id", "type" }` (Appendix A). `richText=html` and `markdown` ask the resolver of `media` once for every media node of the read, populated entries included, and the renderers write its `Url` as `src` and, for a node without `alt`, its `Text`, escaped; a node it leaves out has no `src`. A write asks the resolver which of the targets it adds, in fields, blocks, components and rich text, do not exist in the site, whoever may read them (`FindMissingAsync`), and refuses those with `FCMS2113` (`ValidationErrorCodes.UnknownReference`, 422) on the path of the field that holds them. Targets the entry already had are not asked again, so an entry whose block or rich text still holds an asset deleted with `force=true` (B-66) saves, and without a resolver nothing is checked.

**Reverse relations as built.** A reverse side is metadata only in v1, and no path of a query reads it. A filter, sort, `fields` or `populate` path that names one, on the queried type or through a relation (`filter[articles.title]` on an author, `populate=author.articles` on an article), is refused with `FCMS2210` (400), whose message names the reverse side and the relation field it mirrors. The code is given to a caller who can read that field, the one introspection shows the reverse side to; for any other caller the path is the unknown field it is to them (`FCMS2202`). The entries that point to an entry are a list of their own type filtered by the relation, such as `/article?filter[author][eq]={id}`. Reverse traversal stays additive: a later version can read it with one reference query by `target_id` per level, which `ix_cms_reference_target` serves, and the field and row filtering of population, so a path refused today can start to work.

**Search as built.** `ContentQuery.Search`, `q=` on the Delivery and Management lists, is a seam the search plugin fills, and core has no index. The engine checks the query as usual, then asks `ISearchIndex.SearchAsync` (4.13) with a `SearchRequest` of the type, the text, the locale (empty for a type that is not localized), the site (null for a global type), the status and the fields (`SearchRequest.Fields`: the searchable fields that are not hidden and that the caller may read in the locale, or null when it may read them all), and narrows the query to the document ids it returns, bound as one parameter. The filter, row rule, site, locale, status, sort and page then apply to those entries as to any list, so the index cannot widen what a caller reads, and the total counts the matches the rest of the query lets through. The index is asked once for a list: the reads that follow it, of the locales its entries fall back to and of each locale of a `locale=all` list, name the ids it chose and search nothing, and still hold the grants, row rule, site, state and trash of the locale they read, so they fill in what an entry holds without widening which entries are listed, and the order, `total`, `nextCursor` and `searchTruncated` are those of the one search ([#3469](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3469)). So the refusal below of a caller who may not read every searchable field, under an index that does not match by field, looks at the locale searched alone, and a `locale=all` list is not refused for a limit in one of its other locales. The index returns at most `FluentCms:Query:MaxSearchResults` ids (1,000), its best matches, and the engine reads no more; the order is the query's sort, as relevance ranking is not part of the seam. When the index returns that many, matches beyond them may be missing from the list and its total, and the list says so with `searchTruncated: true` ([#3302](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3302)): a member left out otherwise, as `total` and `nextCursor` are, so that a client that does not know it reads the list as before; the same member in the Delivery and Management lists and the MCP list tools, a `searchTruncated: Boolean!` field of a GraphQL list, and `PagedResult.SearchTruncated` in the SDK. A read of the trash never sets it: it asks the index for drafts, and the search plugin keeps no row of an entry in the trash, so `q=` there finds nothing and the count of the index would measure the drafts out of it. It is set when the index returned the limit, so exactly that many matches also count as cut, and an index that stops short of the limit on its own is not reported. It is never set for a caller who may read none of the searchable fields, whose list is empty whatever the index found. The index narrows its matches to the request's type, locale, site and state before the cap (the search plugin does, `SearchStore.MatchAsync`), so entries of another site or of a state the query does not read neither take a place among the ids nor set the flag; the filter and the row rule apply after the cap, so the flag can count entries the row rule then hides. That is a disclosure the flag adds and the design keeps: a caller under a row rule such as "own entries", none of whose entries match, gets an empty list with `searchTruncated: true` and learns that at least `MaxSearchResults` entries it may not see hold the words in the fields it may read, in its site, locale and state; it learns nothing of other sites, locales, states or fields. The hand-written clients send no `q`, so they do not model the member. A caller who may read none of the searchable fields finds nothing, though the index is still asked, so that it refuses as it refuses others. The engine gives a list only to an index whose `ISearchIndex.MatchesByField` (a default member, false) is true, and refuses `q` with `FCMS2211` to a caller who may not read every searchable field when the index does not say so; a filter refuses a field the caller may not read, and a search leaves it out, so neither tells what it holds ([#3263](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3263)). A text that is blank or longer than 256 characters is refused (`FCMS2204`, `FCMS2207` on `q`). Core fills the slot with `NoSearchIndex`, so until the plugin or the host replaces it, a query with `q=` is refused with `FCMS2211` (400) before anything runs. A single-entry read and a bulk request refuse `q` as they refuse the other list parameters, and the OpenAPI document lists it on every list. The search plugin adds search with `Providers.Use<ISearchIndex, T>()` and no core change (5.7, "Search plugin as built"), and refuses on its own a type without a `Searchable` field (`FCMS1700`) and a site where it is disabled (`FCMS2211`).

**Pagination.** Offset and page with `total` (skip the count with `withTotal=false`). Keyset cursor: base64 of the sort key values plus the document id, which is always appended as the final sort key for stability. A v7 `document_id` makes that key stable, but it does not give creation order within one millisecond.

**Pagination as built.** A total is a second query on every list. The Management API counts unless `withTotal=false`, and Delivery counts only with `withTotal=true`, since a site rarely shows one. `CountAsync` runs the count alone. A page or an offset may skip at most `FluentCms:Query:MaxOffset` entries, 10,000 by default, which is a hundred pages of the largest size: the database reads and drops every skipped row, so a deeper page costs as much as reading that far, while a cursor seeks. A deeper one is refused with `FCMS2207` on `page` or `offset`, and its message points at cursors. A cursor is base64url JSON of the sort values and is neither opaque nor signed. That leaks nothing, since its values were in the page that gave it and decoding binds each one as a parameter, but a client sends it back as it came and does not read or build one, since its form may change; the API reference says so. This answers items 2, 3 and 9 of [#2869](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2869).

**Concurrency.** Each `(document, locale)` has an integer `version`. Writes carry `expectedVersion` (`If-Match: "7"` on REST). A mismatch is rejected with 412 and the current entry in the problem details `current` extension. On a localized type a 412 can also come with the caller's own version unchanged, when a write that moves the shared fields, or adds a locale, finds that another locale of the entry moved since the write read it ("Shared fields and versions as built", below).

**Unique races, as built.** Each of these refusals comes from a check before the write: an existing singleton, a locale the entry already has, a taken `[Unique]` value, a taken site slug. The database enforces the same keys: the singleton key and the published side of `[Unique]` (4.3), `uq_cms_document_document_locale`, and `uq_cms_site_slug`. On SQLite the content service's transaction takes the write lock when it begins, so a second writer waits and its checks see the first. On PostgreSQL and SQL Server under READ COMMITTED, two writers can pass their checks together, and the second then breaks the key at its insert. `IDataProvider.IsUniqueViolation` recognises that error, in each provider package: SQLSTATE 23505 on PostgreSQL, errors 2601 and 2627 on SQL Server, and `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE` or `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_PRIMARYKEY` on SQLite (a primary key counts on the other two as well).
