Its overload with a key of the model (`IUniqueConstraint`) tells that key from the others by what the database reports of the key, never by the part of a message that repeats the duplicate value, and is `false` by default (4.7, [#3447](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3447)). `ContentService` runs a write that meets it once more, in a new transaction and on the context as it was before the first run (what that run left tracked is detached, and the caller's entries go back to the state and values they had), and its checks then see the winner, so a loser gets exactly what a writer that came second gets and never a 500:

- a second singleton: `FCMS2401` (409);
- a locale added twice, as with a stale `expectedVersion`: `VersionConflictException` (412, `FCMS3105`) with the current entry;
- a publish of a value another entry published first: `NotUnique` (`FCMS2111`, 422) on the field.

A deadlock victim runs again the same way, up to five times after a jittered pause, recognised by `IDataProvider.IsDeadlock` (error 1205 on SQL Server, SQLSTATE 40P01 on PostgreSQL, never on SQLite, whose writers queue). Under SQL Server's locking reads two updates of one entry could deadlock between the read and the write of their transactions; `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` (4.3) takes the reads out of that, and the reruns stay for two writers that lock rows in different orders. The victim's next run sees the winner's version and gets `VersionConflictException`, as a sequential loser does ([#2711](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2711)). A write inside a transaction the caller began takes a savepoint; where there is none (SQL Server with MARS), a failed write rolls the caller's whole transaction back, and the transaction stays ended, so a write the caller goes on with, as a hook that caught the failure would, fails as its commit does instead of committing on its own.

A hook that ran before the failed insert runs again in the second run, inside its transaction, unless a check refuses first; what the first run raised is never delivered, since its events and outbox rows went with its rollback. A second unique violation in that run is a key no check covers, and it fails as it is. A write joined to a transaction the service began runs again with it; one inside a transaction the caller began fails with the database error, because only the owner can start over. The draft side of `[Unique]` has no index, so on PostgreSQL and SQL Server two drafts saved together may share a value; each is refused at publish while the other holds it, and an empty value, which drafts do not clash on, goes to whichever publish commits first. `SiteManager.CreateAsync` answers a slug taken between its check and its insert with the error of a taken slug (`FCMS2304` on `slug`). This settles decision 2 of [#2847](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2847): the database enforces the singleton, and a lost race answers as a sequential loser does. Only SQLite has run these paths; the race tests in `FluentCMS.Data.Tests` move to the provider matrix with [#2711](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2711).

**Round trips of a write, as built.** A save reads the entry's documents, its draft rows and references, and the entry as the principal's read shows it, checks each `[Unique]` value, advances the version, replaces the references, and saves the rows and the version in one save. That save writes over the rows the transaction read without reading them again, and has no savepoint of its own, since the service's transaction or savepoint already takes back a save that fails. A check of the entry the service has just found or written in its site does not look for it in that site again (`AccessTarget.Found`), so with no row rule the check before the write sends nothing, and the check after it is not made. The read the principal is shown is made from the documents, draft rows and references the save has just read, so it asks the database only which relation targets are readable; under a row rule on the read it reads the rows. The response reads the entry's rows again, and takes the references the save wrote and the targets the shown read found readable, unless another write ran in the scope since (`KnownEntry`). The reads inside a write use the locale its operation resolved. A read asks once per target type which relation targets, at the top level and nested, the principal may read. The versions of an entry are counted only once its draft version passes the cap, since versions are numbered by it. The entry is copied into JSON once before the hooks and once after each kind of hook that ran, and the version snapshot is the last copy. A save of a `Modified` locale also reads its published rows to decide its status, once its swap holds its row (4.5, "Lock order as built"). A save that changes a shared field reads the draft and published state of each other published locale (4.5, "Modified, as built"). Neither makes a new copy of the entry. `ContentRoundTripTests` holds a typical update to these numbers: on a non-localized type with three relations, blocks and a `[Unique]` slug, an editor with no row rule makes 12 round trips (one and two more on PostgreSQL and SQL Server for the lock timeout, 4.5, "Lock waits as built"), counting a save as one, where it made 33 before [#2863](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2863) and 16 before [#2864](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2864), and copies the entry twice, where it copied it ten times.

**Validation pipeline.** Field type coercion and declarative rules, custom field validators, entry validators, uniqueness (both states, per site and locale), then, on publish, `Required`, `RequiredWhen` and reference integrity. On a draft the content service writes `''` and the empty rich text document for missing `NOT NULL` values (4.3), and publish treats `''` as missing.

**Hook pipeline.** `Before` hooks run inside the transaction in `[HookOrder]` order and may mutate the entry or cancel (`HookRejectedException`, mapped to 422). `After` hooks run inside the transaction after the write so plugin tables stay consistent. Events (4.11) are dispatched after commit. The pipeline resolves and orders the hooks of a kind once per scope and content class, not on every call, so a hook registered as transient lives as long as the scope (item 8 of [#2869](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2869)). Each hook runs for at most `FluentCms:Content:HookTimeout` (30 seconds, at most a day), started on the thread pool so that one that blocks before its first await is bounded too: one that runs longer has its token cancelled and is no longer waited for, and the write fails with `FCMS2410` (503, naming the hook; event 9406) and rolls back, whole in a transaction of its own and to its savepoint in another's, and either way what it left tracked is detached and what the context tracked before it goes back to how it was. A scheduled publish whose hook timed out is tried again, and its last attempt records the failure in a scope of its own. A hook that ends after `SlowHookThreshold` (1 second) is logged (event 9407) ([#3307](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3307)).

**A hook left running, as built ([#3307](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3307)).** A hook that timed out, or that had not ended when its caller was cancelled (a client that went away, a lost idempotency key), may still be running on the scope's services, so it marks the scope, which from then on writes and commits nothing more, with `FCMS2410` naming that hook.

- No further hook runs, the content service refuses every further write, and a write under way neither commits nor releases its savepoint, whatever a hook or other caller made of the failure of a write nested in it. A bulk reports the items after it as `NotRun`.
- A hook left by its cancelled caller is logged with event 9408, naming it, as one that timed out is with 9406 ([#3542](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3542)). The hook may be inside a command on the scope's connection, its reader open, while the write rolls back and the scope is disposed; SqlClient without MARS then refuses the rollback (any command while a reader is open) and throws that out of the transaction's disposal, which Npgsql does not (it swallows a failed rollback) and SQLite does not need to (its rollback beside an open reader succeeds). That refusal is logged with event 9409 and the write still fails with what it failed with, but its transaction and its locks stay until the scope disposes the context, whose closed connection rolls them back; the guard keeps it from committing meanwhile.
- `HookScopeGuard`, an interceptor of the scope's `CmsDbContext`, refuses what is still sent on it, by the hook, by a write nested in it or by a stager: `SaveChanges`, `ExecuteUpdate`, `ExecuteDelete`, `ExecuteSql`, a commit, also of a transaction the caller began, and the release of a savepoint. So nothing commits on its own after the transaction it ran in has ended. Reads and rollbacks go on.
- It cannot cover what does not pass that context as a write: a plugin's own context, `SystemDbContext` or another scope, such as that of a job the hook schedules or of an event it publishes after the rollback; a command made on the connection, a commit of the driver's transaction, or SQL that writes sent as a query (`SqlQuery`, `FromSql`); a statement already sent when the scope was marked; and what the hook does outside the database.

**Response shape.** Flat JSON, system fields at the top level under reserved names, relations inlined by depth:

```json
{
  "id": "0192f2a4-7b1c-7d2e-9f10-2a3b4c5d6e7f",
  "status": "published",
  "locale": "fr-CA",
  "version": 7,
  "createdAt": "2026-11-01T09:12:00Z",
  "updatedAt": "2026-11-01T10:40:00Z",
  "publishedAt": "2026-11-01T10:41:00Z",
  "title": "Bonjour",
  "slug": "bonjour",
  "author": { "id": "0192f2a4-...", "type": "author", "name": "Amir" },
  "tags": [{ "id": "0192f2a4-...", "type": "tag" }],
  "sections": [{ "type": "hero", "id": "V1StGXR8_Z5j", "heading": "..." }],
  "fallbacks": { "subtitle": "fr" }
}
```

Lists return `{ "items": [...], "total": 120, "page": 2, "pageSize": 20, "nextCursor": "..." }`, and `"searchTruncated": true` when a search reached the index's limit (4.4, "Search as built"). A nested `sys` object for system fields was considered and rejected (Q14).

**Pending schedules as built** ([#3094](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3094)). Every entry the Management API answers, single or in a list, carries `scheduledPublishAt` and `scheduledUnpublishAt` after `publishedAt`: the instants of the pending scheduled publish and unpublish of its locale, from the mirror on `cms_document` (4.5), and null where none is pending. Under `locale=all` each locale carries its own. So do the trash, the answer of a write and the `current` of a 412. They are read-only system fields: a write that sends them back ignores them, `fields=` selects them, and a filter or a sort on them is refused for now, since adding one later only adds. The content service answers this Management shape where it is asked for (`ManagementShape`, scoped): the content routes of the Management API ask for it for their request, and an operation of `Origin.Mcp` has it, since the MCP tools answer as those routes do (5.4). Like `createdBy` and `updatedBy`, the two fields are outside the Delivery response shape, so only a principal that manages reads them, and a Delivery key reading through MCP gets neither. Everywhere else, on Delivery, in GraphQL and in what jobs and plugins read, the fields do not exist: a read leaves them out, `fields=` naming them is an unknown field (`FCMS2202`), and `IContentSurface.ReadableFieldsAsync` does not list them. GraphQL has no field for them, since it schedules nothing (5.3). An entry read by id through the fallback chain carries the schedules of the locale that served it, as it carries that locale's status and version.

**Other surfaces as built (B-75).** `IContentSurface` (`FluentCMS.Content`, in the SDK) is what a surface over content that is not REST asks, so that it answers as REST does and cannot drift from it. Core implements it, scoped, with what the REST APIs use: `DefaultPageSize`, `MaxPageSize` and `MaxPopulateDepth` from `FluentCms:Query`; `ReadableFieldsAsync(type, locale)`, the system and content fields of a type that the principal of the operation may read in its site, or null for a type it may not read or that does not exist, from the access answers the query engine asks; and `ToJson(entry)`, the response shape above. For the MCP tools (5.4, B-72) it also writes a list, a publish result and a schedule as the Management API answers them (`ToJson` of a `PagedResult`, a `PublishResult` and a `ScheduleInfo`), reads the parameters of a list (`filter[...]`, `sort`, `pageSize` and the rest) as a `ContentQuery` with `ParseQuery`, and the body of the schedule route with `ParseSchedule`, all through the code of the routes. For the `bulk` tool (5.4, B-74) it writes a `BulkResult` as the bulk route answers it, with the problem of each failed entry. The problem of an exception is `IProblemDescriber`'s (4.13). The GraphQL plugin (5.3) is its first caller.

### 4.5 Versioning, publishing, scheduling and trash

Rows 6.1 to 6.11; decisions 38, 39, 40, 41.

**States.** Per `(document, locale)`: `Draft` (never published), `Published` (draft equals published), `Modified` (draft content differs from published), `Unpublished` (was published, now withdrawn). Status is stored on `cms_document` and recomputed on every transition.

**Modified, as built.** Whether a published locale is `Published` or `Modified` depends only on its content: its draft fields, shared and localized, compared with its published fields, never on version numbers. Save, publish, discard and version restore decide it this way. So does the recompute of the other published locales that follows a change to the shared row. A save of a `Published` locale compares with its draft, which is what is published. A save of a `Modified` locale reads the published rows once its swap holds its row. A save that changes a shared field recomputes the other published locales. An edit that puts back the published value therefore gets the same status, `Published`, whether it comes from a save, a discard or a recompute.

**Transitions.**

| Operation | Effect | Validation | Event |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Save draft | upsert state 0 rows, `draft_version++`, write `cms_version` | draft rules | `EntryDraftSavedEvent` |
| Publish | copy state 0 rows to state 1, set `published_version`, `published_at` | full rules plus reference integrity | `EntryPublishedEvent` |
| Unpublish | delete state 1 rows, status `Unpublished` | permission | `EntryUnpublishedEvent` |
| Discard draft | copy state 1 rows to state 0, as a new version; needs `update` | permission | `EntryDraftDiscardedEvent` |
| Restore version | new draft from snapshot; needs `update` | draft rules | `EntryVersionRestoredEvent` |
| Trash | set `deleted_at`; excluded by the global query filter | permission | `EntryTrashedEvent` |
| Restore from trash | clear `deleted_at` | permission | `EntryRestoredEvent` |
| Purge | delete all rows; separate permission `content.{type}.purge` | | `EntryPurgedEvent` |
| Schedule | row in `cms_schedule`, job enqueued | | `EntryScheduledEvent` |

**Versions.** Every save writes an immutable snapshot (decision 38) with author, origin, client id and an optional message. The author is kept as `cms_activity` keeps the actor: its id, which is empty for the system, its kind and the name it had at the time, so a version saved by a user, a key, a service account, a job or the seed names its author, and renaming a user later leaves its earlier versions as they were ([#3097](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3097)). The author is named to whoever may list versions, which needs the Management scope and a read of the type: such a principal reads `updatedBy`, which a field limit does not hide (it limits content fields, not system fields), so a version names no one its entry would not. Core writes no content for a member, its tokens having no scope that manages, so core never names a member as an author. `JobActor` is public and takes the kind `Member`, so a plugin's job that acts for a member writes versions in its name, and the name is the one the actor carries, as on an activity row; such a plugin gives a display name there, not the member's email address. A per-type cap (default 50, `[PublishPolicy(MaxVersions = n)]`) prunes oldest drafts when a version is written; versions that were published are never pruned, and only a publish marks one so, in `status_after`. Diff is computed on demand as a field-level structural diff (blocks and rich text compared as trees) and returned as a list of `{ path, kind, before, after }`. A list whose items all carry an `id` on both sides, as blocks do ([ADR 0002](../decisions/0002-stable-block-ids.md)), pairs them by id: an item kept in its order is compared at its index in the later version, and one that is new, gone or moved is added at its new index and removed at its old one, so a block inserted at the top is one change. Any other list, such as a repeated component, a multiple select or the content of rich text, pairs by position (item 4 of [#2869](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2869)). A snapshot from before a field was renamed holds it under its former name, and a read, a diff and a restore find it there (4.2, "Former names in versions and validation as built"). A snapshot holds hidden fields, so reads and diffs go through the reader's field limits as they are today, and need the Management scope as well as the draft read. Access to versions is decided on the entry's current row: history is part of the entry, so a reader the row rule lets in also sees versions from before the entry came within the rule. A restore is a write of the principal's own: what it may not write or read, and what is hidden or read-only, keeps the value the draft holds, except for the system principal. A principal that can write a field it cannot read may not discard or restore at all, whatever that field holds, because the status and version those answer with would check a saved guess against the value.

**Trash.** `DeleteAsync` sets `deleted_at` and `deleted_by` on every locale of the entry, and the global query filter on `cms_document` hides it from reads, queries, versions, publishing and reference checks; the content rows, references and versions stay as they were, so a restore brings back the entry as it was. `QueryTrashAsync` reads the trash as drafts through the query engine, in one locale, so the read grant, its row rule, its locale and field limits and the site hold; it needs `restore` or `purge` on the type and lists only what the principal may read. Every entry carries `deletedAt`, and `deletedBy` where the principal may read who changed an entry; both names are reserved (4.2), so they never overwrite a field of the type. The query filter is off for that whole query, so every row reached through a relation says explicitly that it is not in the trash: a trashed target never matches a filter or a row rule and is never populated. A filter on the id of a relation matches only a target the same read would show: of that type, not in the trash, in the site of a site-scoped type, and published on a published read. `PurgeAsync` needs `content.{type}.purge`, deletes the content rows of every state and locale, the references the entry holds and every version of it, published ones included, and raises `EntryPurgedEvent` per locale. It follows the rules of restore: an entry that is not in the trash, in another site or type, or refused by the row rule of the grant is `EntryNotFoundException`, the same for all of them; one the principal reaches in some locales only is `ContentAccessDeniedException`. References from other entries to a purged one are the values of their fields and stay; a read leaves out a target that is not there, and publishing warns with `FCMS2404` as it did for the trashed one. The values the entry held are free again, since uniqueness counted the trash.

**Retention.** `[PublishPolicy(TrashRetentionDays = n)]` (`PublishPolicy(new PublishPolicyOptions { TrashRetentionDays = n })` on the builder, `trashRetentionDays` in schema JSON) sets how long a type keeps its trash; the default is 30 days, the least 1 and the most 36500. The job `content.trash.purge` runs daily at 03:17 UTC from a recurring schedule stored at every startup. Each run purges, per type and site, the entries trashed longer ago than the type keeps them, through `PurgeAsync` as `Principal.System` with `Origin.Job`, one transaction per entry, at most 500 purges per type per run (`Limit` in the payload). Due entries are read oldest first in pages (`PageSize`), so an entry that fails is logged and left for the next run without using up the limit or hiding those behind it, and a type that fails is logged while the others still run. The purge checks inside its transaction that the entry went to the trash before the cutoff, so one trashed again since is left alone. A run logs the number purged and failed. A run that finds nothing due does nothing, so it is safe to run again.

**Reference integrity.** On publish, references to unpublished or trashed entries produce warnings in the response (`warnings[]`) by default; `[PublishPolicy(UnpublishedReferences = Block)]` on a type turns them into a 422. A target counts as published when it has a published row in the entry's locale or its fallback chain. A self-reference is exempt. A cycle is not: under `Block` neither side can go first, and the way out is to publish one side without the reference and set it afterwards.

The answer must not be a way to ask about an entry the principal may not read, since a write takes any id:

| Target, for a principal other than the system principal | Warn | Block |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Published, and it may read it | nothing | publishes |
| Unpublished, and it may read it | `FCMS2403` with the id | `FCMS2403` on the field |
| Trashed, missing, in another site or of another type; refused by its row rule, asked of the locale that serves the target; or with rows only in locales its grant does not cover | `FCMS2404`, no id, all identical | `FCMS2404` on the field |
| Of a type it may not read, or of no type at all | nothing | always one refusal, no path |
| In a field it can write but not read, which is where it chooses the id | nothing | always one refusal, no path |
| In a field it can neither read nor write, or hidden: it did not choose the id | nothing | a refusal with no path, only if the target really is a problem |

The system principal is told the id on `FCMS2404`.

**Reference checks, as built.** Publishing, scheduling, a diff and a version restore ask about an entry's targets per type, not per reference: one query reads the rows of every target of a type, and one for each locale that serves them asks the row rule (`IAuthorizationEvaluator.AccessibleEntriesAsync`), as a read does (4.4). A diff asks about the targets of both versions at once. `ContentRoundTripTests` checks that 2 and 20 references cost the same number of queries, with and without a row rule on the target type.

**Relation targets on a read.** The same rules hold for a top-level relation, a relation inside a block or component, an entry link inside rich text, and a relation in the `data` of an inline block, which also count in `cms_reference` and reference integrity. A target the principal may not read by type or row rule, in another site, of another type than the field's, or with rows only in locales its grants do not cover, is returned as null or left out of a list. A row rule is asked only of the locale being read, so where that locale has no rule, only grants with no rule count as covering a locale: a rule on another locale's grant cannot vouch for a row there, and such a target is left out even if the principal could read it directly in that locale. A rich text link keeps its text and loses its target, and a link to an unknown type is treated as one to an unreadable type. A published read shows only what is published: a target that is trashed or has no published row in any locale goes too, and one published only in another locale stays `{ id, type }`, since a reader with a fallback can follow it. A draft read keeps a trashed target of a type the principal may read as a reference, because that is what its author attached. A filter on the relation still matches a target left out: it answers about the source entry and a value the caller named.

A write answers what a read showed, so what the read left out stays: a nested target goes back in its block, and a rich text document keeps its links when it comes back as it was read. Rich text nodes have no ids, so an edited document loses the links the principal could not see. Keeping must not test whether the target exists, or a later warning would say so: a principal keeps every target its read left out, whatever the id is. Only one whose read grant on that type has no row rule and no locale limit, which a read tells anyway, loses a target that does not exist in that type and site, trashed counting as existing. So a limited principal cannot clear a ghost id, and under `Block` the entry stays refused until a wider principal does. The system principal keeps nothing it did not send.

**Read-only values sent back.** A full update may send back a top-level field the principal can see but not change: it is no write while it is the same as the read showed. The same holds inside a component, a block, the `data` of an inline block and an inline block inside a block, on an update and a patch alike, since such a field is sent whole: a read-only value that is the same as the read showed in the stored item it pairs with is taken out of the write, as if the client had left it out, and the stored value stays. A single component is its own pair. The items of a block list or a repeated component, and the inline blocks of a document, pair as the write keeps their hidden values ([ADR 0002](../decisions/0002-stable-block-ids.md)). A block or an inline block sent with its `id` pairs with the stored block of the same id and type anywhere in the same top-level field, so it may move into or out of a nested list or the document of another inline block. One whose id is not stored is new and pairs with none. An item without an id, as every item of a repeated component is, pairs within its list or document among the stored items whose ids the write does not send: first one of the same type with the same visible content, then the next one of that type in order. A block then takes the id of its pair. So a read-only value that moves with its block, or stays on a block whose neighbour was added or removed, is no change, and one that is not the value of the stored item its item pairs with is refused (`FCMS1406`): kept in place while the content around it moved, sent on an added item that pairs with none, set where its pair has none, or sent as null where its pair has one. A client that edits blocks may leave the read-only values out, and they follow their blocks as hidden values do. The comparison is with what the read showed, so the answer tells the principal nothing it could not read, and a value that names a hidden field is never the same. Only a field the principal may write is looked into, and a new locale compares only shared fields, since a localizable field has nothing in that locale to show. A client that drops ids still gets a guess. A block moved and edited without its id, or a new block without one in a write that removes a block of its type, may take the read-only and hidden values of another block, whether or not they are sent. A client that wants a block treated as new sends an id of its own.

**Shared fields.** Non-localizable fields live on one row per state, so publishing one locale publishes them for every published locale. A grant limited to some locales may publish only when those fields have not changed, or no other locale is published. The other locales' status is recomputed after a transition that moves the shared row. So `published_version` names the version a locale last published, and what that locale serves can differ from that snapshot in its shared fields.

**Shared fields and versions as built ([#3265](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3265)).** A write that changes a shared field changes every locale's draft, and adding a locale writes the shared row as the write read it, so either advances the draft version of each other locale of the entry by a compare and swap from the version the write read, and gives that locale a version of its draft as it now is, with the message `Shared fields changed in {locale}.` or `Added the locale {locale}.` Every swap of one write, its own locale's included, is taken in one order, ordinal by locale code, so two writes that cross wait for each other instead of deadlocking, and the second gets 412. The written locale's row is locked in its place in that order and moved after the others, so a 412 at any of the swaps names that locale and shows it as stored: at the version the winner gave it or added it at, or at the caller's own when only another locale moved. A locale being added that lost to another locale's change is not stored yet, so its 412 has no `current`. Another locale's swap moves its version, `updatedAt` and `updatedBy` alone: its status is decided afterwards from its row read again, so a publish or unpublish that committed in between keeps its status, and a pending scheduled publish of that locale takes the new version, the shared hash still deciding whether it is skipped. A client that read another locale before the change gets 412 on its next write or publish of it, with the current entry, and so does a write whose own version still holds when another locale moved since its read, so no shared change is lost and a publish never copies a shared row it did not read. Saves, discards, version restores, a publish whose hooks change the draft and adding a locale do this. An import of an entry the instance has advances the locales the package does not name in the same way; the locales it names take the version numbers it writes. The extra versions count against `MaxVersions` like any other and can prune a locale's older ones, and no event announces them, so a version or ETag a client keeps from an event goes stale. No column was added: the versions of the locales are the stamp. A publish reads the draft again before it copies and is refused when it differs ([#3536](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3536)): with 412 and the stored version when that locale's version grew and this transaction did not move it since the publish began, which at read committed is another request's commit, and with `FCMS2405` otherwise, which is a write of a hook. A swap of the publish that loses, where it takes its own row or where it writes over the draft for what its hooks changed, answers the same way ([#3546](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3546)): `FCMS2405` when this transaction moved that locale since the publish began, as a hook that saved the draft does even with the values it had, and otherwise 412 with the stored version, so a 412 never names a version that rolled back with the publish. A publish in a transaction of its own rolls back before it reads that version and entry, so the 412 shows nothing it did not commit; one inside another transaction reads them as that transaction sees them. In a scope that left a hook running the refusal is `FCMS2410` before either.

**Lock order as built ([#3524](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3524)).** Every path that writes an entry takes the `cms_document` rows of the entry it will write first, one statement each, by primary key and ordinal by locale code, and only then the entry's content rows of either state, its references and versions, and last its schedules and their jobs; a row of an earlier kind is written afterwards only once it is held, as the status of another locale or the mirror of a schedule is. So two writes, publishes or scheduled jobs of one entry that cross wait for each other instead of deadlocking, where PostgreSQL and SQL Server ended them by deadlock detection and a rerun after about one and five seconds. Every statement that locks one row of `cms_document` finds it by its primary key, which is the clustered key on SQL Server: found through `uq_cms_document_document_locale`, SQL Server locks that index's key first, so a writer that waited for the row held the key, and the holder's next statement on the row, through the same index, waited for it, a deadlock that PostgreSQL, which locks the row alone, did not have. The statements of many rows by document id (a trash, a restore, a purge) come only once those rows are held. A save takes its own row by its swap, and every row of the entry when it moves the shared fields or adds a locale. A publish holds its own row at the version it read and every other row of the entry before it copies, since the copy of the shared row decides the status of every locale published by then; so does a discard, which writes what is published, and which is a conflict (412 with the version it read) when its row was published, unpublished or given another status between its read and its holds. An unpublish holds every row of the entry before it unpublishes and reads under them whether it is the last published locale, which decides whether the shared published row goes. A schedule and a cancel hold the row of their locale once they have reached the entry, and a schedule reads it again under the hold. A scheduled job holds every row of the entry and reads its schedule again under them, an import holds every row of the entry before it writes the locales of the package in the package's order, and a trash, a restore and a purge hold each row before the one statement that marks or deletes them all, in whatever order the database takes them. Those that hold rows they read before their transaction held any (a trash, a restore, a purge, a scheduled job, an import) read the entry's rows again under the holds; a locale added in between runs the write again in a transaction of its own, which then reads, checks and announces it, and elsewhere is a 412 that a client retries. A hold is an update that sets nothing new. The site row is outside the order: a site deletion locks it and no row of an entry, so a write that adds a row takes its shared lock where it adds it. What a hook writes, and a caller's transaction that writes more than one entry, take their rows where they write them. A write decides its own status before its swap, from the row it read, and decides it again once the swap holds the row: the swap compares the published version and the status too, so a publish or an unpublish of that locale since the read is found and the status decided from the row as it then is, and a locale read `Modified` reads its published rows then, so another locale's publish of the shared row is seen. A publish whose row went to the trash meanwhile is not found (`EntryNotFoundException`, 404), as a publish of an entry in the trash is. The holds cost a statement per row that the path did not write before: a publish and a discard one for their own row and one for each other locale, an unpublish one per locale with a read of the entry's rows, a schedule one with a read of the entry's rows and a cancel one, a scheduled job one per locale with a read of the entry's rows and of its schedule, and an import, a trash, a restore and a purge one per locale with a read of the entry's rows. A save sends no more than before, and one whose published version or status moved under it sends one hold, one read and one swap more; a version restore of a `Modified` locale reads its published rows once more, after the swap. A mirror of a schedule is found by a read before it is written. The holds also make writes wait: while a publish, a discard, an unpublish, a scheduled publish or unpublish or an import of an entry runs, its hooks included, a write to any locale of that entry waits for it, where a write of another locale did not wait for a publish before. A hook runs for at most `HookTimeout` (30 seconds), so a wait can outlast what a client waits for; a write gives up at its lock timeout and answers `FCMS2411` (503, below).

**Lock waits as built ([#3573](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3573)).** A write in a transaction of its own begins it through `IDataProvider.BeginWriteAsync` with `FluentCms:Content:LockTimeout` (10 seconds, at most a day), so a statement that waits that long for what another transaction holds gives up with the provider's lock timeout error, which `IDataProvider.IsLockTimeout` recognises by its number and never by its message: on PostgreSQL `SET LOCAL lock_timeout` in the transaction, which ends with it, and SqlState `55P03`; on SQL Server `SET LOCK_TIMEOUT`, sent alone as a batch since inside `sp_executesql` it would end with that call, and set back to `-1` as the last statement before the commit, and before the rollback of a transaction that did not commit, on the driver's connection, so that a context whose connection stays open keeps no bound after the write (a connection that fails the set back, broken or held by a hook left running, is reset when the pool opens it again, as at a new login), and error `1222`; on SQLite the busy wait of `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, whose command timeout Microsoft.Data.Sqlite takes from the connection's `DefaultTimeout`, lowered to the lock timeout in whole seconds for that statement alone and never raised, and `SQLITE_BUSY` or `SQLITE_LOCKED` (`IsBusy`). On a SQLite file each try of the statement also waits up to the busy timeout of `SqliteWriteAheadLog` (`PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000`) inside SQLite, and Microsoft.Data.Sqlite looks at the time only between tries, so there the bound is the lock timeout rounded up to those 5 second steps: 10 seconds is waited as 10 to 15, and 1 second as about 5; a database in memory has no busy timeout and ends within 150 ms of the bound. SQLite locks the database and not a row, so there the wait is for the one write lock, which any other connection's write transaction holds, of whatever entry or table; a write that has begun waits for no other writer, and its commit keeps the command timeout. The default is a third of the drivers' 30 second command timeout, so that the lock wait, and not the command, ends a wait, with room for the statement, and ten times `SlowHookThreshold`, so that only a holder far slower than a write should be is given up on; a command timeout below the lock timeout ends the wait first, on PostgreSQL and SQL Server with the driver's timeout error, answered 500 `FCMS3199` as before, and on SQLite, where both bound the same busy wait, with `FCMS2411` at the connection's `Default Timeout`. Site deletion, which locks the site row in a transaction of its own, and the reads outside a write's transaction, such as the read of the answer after the commit, are not bounded by the lock timeout and still end at the command timeout. The content service turns that error into `EntryBusyException`, at its own transaction and at a write nested in another's, and does not run the write again, as it does a deadlock victim: the holder may hold on for as long as its hooks run, so the caller decides when to try. The write rolled back whole, or to its savepoint inside another transaction, so nothing of it is stored, no outbox row or version is written, no event is raised, and an `Idempotency-Key` is released as for any refusal. A deadlock victim is unchanged: it runs again up to five times. REST answers 503 `FCMS2411` (`Entry busy`) with `Retry-After: 1`, since the retry waits for the holder again up to the lock timeout; a bulk item fails with `FCMS2411` and the others go on; an import reports the item; a scheduled job's attempt fails and the runner tries it again; GraphQL and MCP describe it through `IProblemDescriber` as `FCMS2411` with status 503, as they do `FCMS2410`. A write inside a caller's transaction runs under that transaction's own bound, the lock timeout of the service's transaction when it is nested in one. A transaction costs a statement more on PostgreSQL and two more on SQL Server, so `ContentRoundTripTests` holds a typical update to 13 and 14 round trips there and 12 on SQLite. `IDataProvider.BeginWriteAsync` and `IsLockTimeout` have defaults that set no bound and recognise nothing, so another provider waits until its command timeout as before. `LockWaitTests` holds an entry's row past a one second lock timeout on every provider (about 5 seconds on a SQLite file) and finds the write refused and the entry, its versions and the outbox as they were; on PostgreSQL and SQL Server it also finds no bound left on a connection that stays open, after a write that committed and one that gave up. `LockWaitEndpointsTests` holds a publish in its after hook on a SQLite file and finds a patch answered 503 with `Retry-After` and the problem body, and the same patch committed once the publish is done. On SQLite `LockWaitTests` also finds a connection whose `Default Timeout` is below the lock timeout give up at it and keep it.

**Scheduling.** `cms_schedule (id, document_id, locale, action, run_at_utc, time_zone, status, job_id, created_by)`. The job runner executes `ScheduledPublishJob` and `ScheduledUnpublishJob`; `cms_document` mirrors the next scheduled times for list views. Cancel and reschedule update the row and the job.

**Schedules as built.** `ScheduleAsync(type, id, ScheduleRequest, LocaleOptions?)` and `CancelScheduleAsync(type, id, action, LocaleOptions?)`, per locale and action. A request is an instant, `ScheduleRequest.At(action, DateTimeOffset)`, or a wall-clock time with an IANA zone, `ScheduleRequest.AtLocalTime(action, DateTime, timeZone)`, whose `DateTime.Kind` is ignored; a Windows zone id is refused (`FCMS2108` on `timeZone`), and the zone's rules on that day give the instant, a time the clocks skip is refused (`FCMS2408`), a time they repeat runs the first time, and a time not in the future is refused (`FCMS2407`). `cms_schedule` also holds `version` (the draft version when scheduled), `created_at`, the `claims` of the scheduling principal, `completed_at` and `error`. `status` is `pending`, `executed`, `cancelled`, `skipped` or `failed`; at most one row is pending per document, locale and action, so a reschedule moves it, and the others stay as history. `cms_document.schedule_publish_at` and `schedule_unpublish_at` mirror the pending times of that locale, and every entry a Management read answers carries them as `scheduledPublishAt` and `scheduledUnpublishAt` (4.4). A schedule, a reschedule and a cancel set or clear the mirror in their transaction, and a schedule that runs, is skipped (by its job, or at once by the save that makes it stale) or fails clears it, so the next read shows each.

The schedule and its `cms_job` row are written in the transaction of the call, so there is never one without the other, and cancel and reschedule remove the job if it has not been claimed. The row is what counts: a job runs only if its schedule is pending and names that job, and it claims the schedule with a guarded update, so a cancelled or moved schedule is never run by the job it left behind, and two workers run it once. A schedule needs the permission, row rule, locale, site and field limits of doing it now, and for a publish under `Block` the reference rules of the principal, with a target that is not published yet let through, since it may be by then. An entry the principal cannot reach, or a request that is wrong, answers the same whatever the id, and cancel does not tell a hidden entry with a schedule from one without.

The jobs `content.schedule.publish` and `content.schedule.unpublish` run as the system principal with the scheduler as `OperationContext.Actor`, so versions, events and the activity log name that user and the origin is `Job`. Before it acts the job reads the scheduler's claims again, looks up the grants of its roles as they are now, and applies the checks of a schedule in full, the reference rules included, so a role that has lost the permission, or been deleted, publishes nothing. A refusal is final: the schedule is `failed` with the codes and paths of the refusal, and the job succeeds. Any other failure is retried by the runner, and recorded as `failed` when the last attempt is used, unless the attempt was stopped by the job's own token. A job the runner dead-letters some other way (an attempt that ran out of time on the last try, a worker that died) is heard through `JobDeadLetteredEvent` by an in-process handler, which closes its schedule as `failed` and clears the mirror (an outbox handler would give every host an outbox; a crash between the settling of the job and the event leaves the schedule pending and its job in the dead-letter list, until the job retention removes the job and first fails the schedule with an error that says so, [#3407](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3407)). Each job has an attempt timeout of five minutes, so one that hangs frees its worker and is retried, and on its last attempt is dead-lettered with `FCMS2508` and its schedule failed ([#3408](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3408)). A `failed` or `skipped` schedule is final: bringing its job back from the dead-letter list finds the schedule closed and does nothing, and the entry must be scheduled again. An entry in the trash is `skipped` (one restored before it is due still runs), a purge removes its schedules and their jobs, and a publish of an entry that is already published or an unpublish of one that is not is `skipped`. A scheduled publish publishes the draft version that was scheduled (`version`), not whatever the draft has become: if the draft has been saved since, or a field shared by every locale has (`shared_hash` holds a hash of those fields when a publish is scheduled, since a save through another locale leaves this locale's version as it was), the schedule is `skipped` with `FCMS2409`, the mirror is cleared and nothing is published, so an editor who may only update cannot have text published under another's name; the entry is scheduled again to publish the new draft. The write that moves the draft does this at once rather than leave a publish that will be skipped: an update, a patch, a discard or a version restore ends a pending scheduled publish of its locale, and that of another locale when it changed the shared fields, as `skipped` with `FCMS2409`, removes its job and clears the mirror in its own transaction, and raises `EntryScheduleCancelledEvent`, so the activity log has a `content.schedule_cancelled` row naming the editor. A write that fails leaves the schedule as it was (item 1 of [#2869](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2869)). An edit to another locale's own fields does not stop it. The job holds every row of the entry from its check to its commit, so a draft is not saved after the check: such a write waits for the job, and the publish still passes the scheduled version as `expectedVersion`. An unpublish does not depend on the draft. A schedule holds the row of its locale before it reads the version and shared hash it records, and the job reads its schedule again once it holds the entry's rows, so a locale added while a publish is scheduled, or due, either commits first and is read, or waits and moves the pending schedule's version with the others, and the schedule publishes at its time rather than being skipped as changed ([#3534](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3534)). Cancelling a schedule needs the checks that creating it needed (the grant's field limits as well), after the same reach as any call, so it tells nothing of an entry the principal cannot read. An admin user's roles are read from `cms_user_role` as they are when the job runs, and a deactivated user holds none; the claims kept at scheduling still give the scopes and the claims row rules read, and the roles of a principal that is not a stored user, such as an API key until keys are stored.

**Agent-safe publishing.** Nothing in core treats `Origin.Mcp` differently: publish needs `content.{type}.publish` for everyone. The safety comes from the MCP plugin exposing publish as a separate tool and from roles given to agent principals. Origin is recorded on every version and activity row, so agent writes are filterable.

### 4.6 Localization and sites in core

Rows 7.1 to 7.6, 7.8, 7.10 to 7.12 (model and API parts); decisions 42 to 45.

**Tables.** `cms_site (id, name, slug, default_locale, is_default, created_at)`, `cms_site_locale (site_id, code, position, fallbacks json, enabled)`. The first system migration seeds the default site with locale `en`.

**Locale resolution.** `locale` parameter or `X-Cms-Locale` header, else the site's default. `locale=all` returns every locale of an entry as a map. No `Accept-Language` negotiation, to keep Delivery responses cacheable by URL.

**Fallback.** Per site an ordered chain (`fr-CA` to `fr` to `en`), applied per field on read; `fallbacks` in the response names the locale that served each fallen-back field; `fallback=none` disables. Lists filter on the requested locale only (decision 44), and search in it only: a field falls back to a locale whose text does not hold the words ([#3469](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3469)).

**Lists of every locale as built ([#3277](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3277)).** A list with `locale=all` holds each entry the caller may read once, whatever locales it has. Its anchor is its row in the first locale, the site's default and then the others in the site's order, that has a row the caller may read, under that locale's row rule; the filter, the sort, the cursor, the page and `total` are those of the anchors, in one query that reads ids alone. Each locale's part of the filter is compiled as the caller reads that locale, and a sort key must be one the caller may sort by in each locale it reads, so a field it may read in one of them and not another is refused, the message naming the locale. The page then reads each locale's rows of its entries, and leaves out a locale the caller may not read. A search (`q`) runs in the first locale the caller may read, and the index knows no row rule, so with a search the anchor is the entry's row in that locale alone, under its rule: a hit on a row the rule hides is not listed, whatever the other locales hold. The other locales of an entry listed are read by its id and need not hold the words ([#3469](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3469)).

**Populate cap across locales as built ([#3284](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3284)).** `MaxPopulatedEntries` counts every related entry a read inlines: the locales of a `locale=all` read and the locales a read falls back to share one count, so the cap holds for the whole answer.

**Sites.** `site_id` is on `cms_document`, `cms_*` content tables, roles, API keys, settings and activity rows. Resolution order: API key binding, `X-Cms-Site` header, `site` parameter, hostname (multi-site plugin), default site. Content types are site-scoped by default; `[Collection("tag", Scope = ContentScope.Global)]` makes one entry set shared by all sites (`site_id` null) and allowed as a relation target from any site.

**Management endpoints.** Sites and locales CRUD live in core (`/api/management/v1/sites`, `/api/management/v1/sites/{id}/locales`) because the data model is core; the multi-site plugin adds hostnames and anything beyond the default site (Q6a).

**Site and locale caching as built.** Every request resolves its site and locale before its own work, so `SiteCache` keeps, on each instance, the default site, the site of each slug, that a site exists, and each site's locales (its default and its `cms_site_locale` rows), for 30 seconds (`SiteCache.CacheDuration`). A write through `SiteManager` (creating or deleting a site, a site's name or default locale, adding, changing or removing a locale, `EnsureLocaleAsync`) drops all of it at once, and so does a `SiteUpdatedEvent` raised on the instance, which is how a plugin that changes a site, such as the multi-site plugin, does the same; a read that was under way during the write is not kept. What is not found is never kept, so a site that another instance created is found on the next request, except that the site of a host name is gone ("Host names as built", below), since that id never names a site again, for about 1,000 ids at the most. No signal reaches another instance (an outbox delivery runs on one instance only), so another instance sees a change to an existing site or its locales when its copy expires, 30 seconds at the most, as plugin enablement (4.13) and settings already do. This answers [#2857](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2857) of the review of 7 Nov 2026 ([#2847](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2847)); the round trips it saves are in 4.10.

**Locale changes as built.** A site's locales decide its fallbacks, so adding a locale, changing its position, whether it is enabled or its fallbacks, and removing it raise `SiteUpdatedEvent` after the commit, as a change to the site's name or default locale does, whether through the Management API or `EnsureLocaleAsync`, which the host and the seeder call. Its outbox handler evicts the site's Delivery copies and raises `CacheInvalidatedEvent` (4.10), and a webhook on `site.updated` sees the change. A seeder may add a locale while other instances serve the site, so `EnsureLocaleAsync` raises the event too. A `PATCH` that leaves the locale as it was raises nothing and writes no row, and neither does `EnsureLocaleAsync` for a locale the site has. The activity log has the `site.locale-added`, `site.locale-updated` or `site.locale-removed` row, which names the locale, beside the event's `site.updated` row. This answers [#2932](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2932).

**Sites beyond the default as built (B-80).** `ISiteAdministration` (`FluentCMS.Sites`, in the SDK) is how a plugin reaches sites, since it references no table of core (3.4). Core implements it over `SiteManager`: `FindAsync(site)` reads a site as `SiteInfo` (the record the Management API answers, now in the SDK), `CreateAsync(name, slug, defaultLocale)` creates one with its default locale and its Public role, and `DeleteAsync(site)` deletes one, with the refusals core has (`FCMS2301` to `FCMS2304`, `FCMS2311`; a name is 1 to 128 characters). Each call needs `sites.manage` for the instance, which a role of one site or a grant limited to sites does not hold, and runs inside an operation. A create raises `SiteCreatedEvent` with the new site as its source, and a delete the new `SiteDeletedEvent` with no site as its source, since no one could read the log of a site that is gone; their activity rows are `site.created` and `site.deleted`. The multi-site plugin maps the routes (5.6). The outbox delivery of `SiteDeletedEvent` evicts the site's Delivery copies (`cache.site.deleted`), as `SiteUpdatedEvent` does, and each first-party plugin removes what it keeps for the site (5.6, "Deleted sites as built"). A delete locks the site's row first (`IDataProvider.LockSiteAsync`: `FOR UPDATE` on PostgreSQL, an `XLOCK` hint on SQL Server, SQLite's one writer), so it waits for every write that is adding a document row to the site, which takes the same row shared (`FOR KEY SHARE`, a `REPEATABLEREAD` hint); then it deletes the row in one statement with `NOT EXISTS` over `cms_document`, and stages `SiteDeletedEvent` in the outbox in that transaction, so the cleanups run even when the process stops right after the commit. A write that adds an entry, or a locale of one, to a site that is gone is refused with `FCMS2301`, whatever an instance's `SiteCache` still holds ([#3306](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3306)).

**Plugin writes beside a site's deletion, a known gap ([#3485](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3485)).** The lock of [#3306](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3306) covers `cms_document` only. A plugin's row of a site is added through a save of its `PluginDbContext<T>` after a check of the site some time before, if any, and with no lock on `cms_site`, so a row added on an instance whose `SiteCache` still has a deleted site, or after the plugin's cleanup of `SiteDeletedEvent` ran, stays as a row of a site that is gone. A hold of the site's row for such a save, taken by core for every plugin's context, is proposed in a pull request of its own, for the maintainer to decide, since it changes what every plugin's save does; a member of the SDK that a plugin calls is the alternative ([#3586](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3586)). The writes that carry a site, and what guards each today:

| Table | Written by | Guard today |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `cms_document` and the content tables | the content service | the shared hold of #3306 |
| `cms_site_locale`, `cms_role` (and its grants), `cms_setting`, `cms_plugin_site` | core | a foreign key to `cms_site` that cascades: the deletion removes them, and a later insert fails on the key |
| `cms_media_asset`, `cms_media_folder`, `cms_media_preset` | uploads, folders, presets, the media import | the site of the request, resolved through `SiteCache`; with `Shared` the library is the default site's, which is never deleted |
| `cms_media_variant` | an image transform | the live asset; a variant made after the cleanup removed the asset is removed by `media.variants.cleanup` (daily) |
| `cms_webhooks_subscription` | `POST /webhooks` | the site of the request |
| `cms_webhooks_delivery` | the delivery job | its webhook's row; one written after the cleanup stays until `webhooks.retention` removes it with the delivery log |
| `cms_mcp_instruction`, `cms_mcp_prompt` | the MCP management endpoints | the site of the request |
| `cms_multi_site_host` | `POST /sites/{id}/hosts` | `ISiteAdministration.FindAsync` before the save, without a lock |
| `cms_search_entry` | the outbox handler and the rebuild job | the site of the event or the job; a deleted site's content, gone before its site, indexes nothing |
| `cms_ai_usage` | an AI action | the site of the request; the rows stay as a log until `UsageRetention` (90 days) |
| `cms_activity`, `cms_outbox`, `cms_job`, `cms_idempotency_key` | core, outside the content transaction | none, by design: a log, a queue and keys that their retention jobs remove by age, and that a site that is gone reads nothing from |
| `cms_api_key` (`site_ids`) | `ApiKeyService` | a check of the sites before the write; a key bound to a site that is gone is refused when it names it, and the binding is not cleaned up |
| MCP clients (`cms_oidc_application`) | core | a client carries no site, only a role, and a role of the site goes with the site |

A media file is written before its row, so an upload or an import whose save fails deletes the file it wrote; a file whose deletion fails is logged (9260) and stays in the storage, which nothing sweeps for files no row names. A file whose row committed before the deletion is deleted by the media cleanup of the site (5.1), and one whose row is added after the cleanup stays with its row.

**Host names as built (B-80).** The order is as above: API key binding, `X-Cms-Site`, `site`, host name, default site. A key bound to several sites that names none reads the site of the host name when that is among them (4.10). A host name that no site has reads the default site, and so does one whose site no longer exists: a key, a header or a parameter that names no site is refused (`FCMS2301`), but a resolver may still hold the host of a site that was deleted, so that is not an error. That such a site is gone is kept in `SiteCache` as that a site exists is, for 30 seconds or until a write on the instance, so a request on its host name reads no site row in that time ([#3009](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3009)). It is kept for the host name only, since a site's id is never given again and a key, a header or a parameter could name any number of ids. A resolver could give any number too, since `ISiteHostResolver` promises no bounded set, so `SiteCache` keeps about 1,000 such ids at the most (`SiteCache.MaxGoneSites`): once it holds that many and none has expired, the next id is not kept and its request reads the site row, so the cache stays bounded whatever a resolver gives. The bound is soft, since requests that store such an id at the same moment can each pass the check and go over it by their number (B-106, [#3051](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3051)). A request asks for the site of its host name once, through `RequestBridge.HostSiteAsync`, which keeps it on the request: the Delivery output cache puts it in its key (4.10) and the endpoint reads the same site. `GET /api/management/v1/me/sites` lists the sites in which the caller holds a grant, the default site first, as `{ "id", "name", "slug", "defaultLocale", "isDefault", "locales" }`, so the admin shows a user only its sites (row 7.13). `locales` is each locale of the site as `{ "code", "position", "enabled" }` in the order of its position, so an editor without `sites.manage` gets a locale switcher and its missing translations (rows 7.7 and 7.9, [#3096](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3096)); the fallbacks stay on `GET /sites/{id}/locales`, which still needs `sites.manage`. Whether a locale is written right to left is not answered: the admin derives it from the code (`CultureInfo.TextInfo.IsRightToLeft`), as it does for its own interface culture. A grant counts when its role is the site's or the instance's and its own site limit, the binding of a key and the role of an MCP client reach the site, whatever it permits there, so a user with roles in two sites reads both without `sites.manage`.

### 4.7 Identity and the authorization server

Rows 4.1, 4.2, 4.9 to 4.11, 4.13, 4.15, 4.16, 2.12, 2.13; decisions 7, 30, 33.

**Foundation.** ASP.NET Core Identity with `CmsUser` and `CmsRole` (GUID keys) in `cms_user*` and `cms_role*` tables, and OpenIddict server with its stores in `cms_oidc_*`.

**Endpoints.** OpenIddict defaults: `/connect/authorize`, `/connect/token`, `/connect/userinfo`, `/connect/introspect`, `/connect/revoke`, `/connect/logout`, `/.well-known/openid-configuration`. Flows: Authorization Code with PKCE for public clients (admin, MCP clients, CLI), rotating refresh tokens, client credentials for service accounts. Access tokens are JWTs signed with keys from a configured certificate or, when none is configured, a generated key persisted in `cms_signing_key` through Data Protection. Audiences: `fluentcms:management` for admins and keys, `fluentcms:members` for members.

**Authorization server as built.** OpenIddict 7 with its EF Core stores on the system model in `cms_oidc_application`, `cms_oidc_authorization`, `cms_oidc_scope` and `cms_oidc_token` (GUID keys, snake_case columns), and `cms_signing_key`. The endpoints are in the `Connect` route group, which takes the `auth` rate limit, the Management and the Members CORS origins and the security headers; discovery and the key set are at `/.well-known/openid-configuration` and `/.well-known/jwks` in a `WellKnown` group with the same origins. PKCE is required of every client, not only public ones, and only `S256` is offered. A refresh token rotates, and a used one is refused once OpenIddict's reuse leeway of 30 seconds is over, which lets two refreshes that cross, as two tabs of one application make, both succeed; a reuse after it revokes the session's tokens. A zero leeway was the first choice and was given up (#2854), since it signed users out on such a race. The token endpoint reads the user again for a code or a refresh token and refuses one who is deactivated or must change the password, or whose security stamp, which codes and refresh tokens carry, has changed, so a password change or reset ends them. Lockout is no reason there, nor at the authorize endpoint over a live cookie: anyone who knows a user name can raise it, so it holds for a new sign-in only, and deactivation and a new stamp are what end a session ([#3271](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3271)); the same change revokes the user's sessions, so its access tokens stop at the next request too (Admin users as built). A bearer token is read from the `Authorization` header only. Access tokens are signed, not encrypted, JWTs with the audience `fluentcms:management`, and carry `sub`, `name`, `fcms_kind` (`AdminUser`, or `ServiceAccount` for client credentials, whose `sub` is the client's id), `scope`, `client_id` and, for a service account only, `roles`; a user's roles are read from `cms_user_role` on each request as before. A user's and a member's access tokens also carry `auth_time`, which a refresh keeps: for a token of the code flow the time of the sign-in on the hosted pages that it goes back to, of either pool, and for a member's direct login (`grant_type=member_password`) the time of the grant, which starts its own session ([#3372](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3372)). `fluentcms:members` is the audience of a member's tokens, and the bearer scheme accepts both (members as built). Scopes are `openid`, `profile`, `email`, `offline_access`, `draft`, `management` and `mcp`, and narrow what roles allow as 4.8 says. The `FluentCms` scheme's bearer scheme is OpenIddict's validation scheme on the local server, which rewrites a valid token's principal to those claims and checks the token and its authorization in the database on each request, so a revoked session stops its access tokens at once. The request bridge sets `OperationContext.ClientId` from `client_id`, and a request from the client named by `cms.Clients.AdminClientId` (`fluentcms-admin` by default) runs with `Origin.Admin`. A refused credential keeps its handler's 401 code over HTTP (`FCMS3016` or `FCMS3019`), and `FCMS3102` otherwise. Tokens are signed with `FluentCms:Security:SigningCertificate` (a PKCS#12 file with an RSA key; `FCMS3022` when it cannot be read) or else with a generated RSA key kept in `cms_signing_key` and protected with Data Protection, which is what `ISigningKeyStatus` reports for `FCMS1102`; the symmetric key that encrypts codes and refresh tokens is always generated and kept there. On Windows the signing certificate, and the key ring certificate of the reference host (`FluentCms:DataProtection:Certificate`), are loaded with `X509KeyStorageFlags.EphemeralKeySet`, so their private keys stay in memory; with the default flags each load wrote a key file to the user's key folder (`%APPDATA%\Microsoft\Crypto\Keys`) that a killed process left behind ([#3212](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3212)). Linux writes none either way and macOS has no ephemeral keys, so the default flags stay there. Kestrel's own HTTPS certificate is Kestrel's to load, and TLS on Windows needs a key that is not ephemeral. The first key of each use has a fixed id, so instances that start together on an empty table insert one row and the others read it; the primary key settles that race with no wait, so it does not take `IDistributedLock` (4.15). The Data Protection key ring is in `cms_data_protection_key` under the application name `FluentCMS` unless the host persists it elsewhere. Clients are registered at start from `cms.Clients.Applications`, bound from `FluentCms:Clients:Applications:{clientId}` (`FCMS3021` for a bad one): a client with redirect URIs uses the code flow and refresh tokens, one with a secret is confidential and may use client credentials with the instance-wide roles it names, a listed client is replaced by its options at each start, one the options registered and no longer list is deleted with its sessions and tokens and a warning, unless the options list none at all, and others are left alone. Consent is implicit for these clients unless one sets `RequireConsent` (see the hosted pages as built). The authorize endpoint challenges the Identity application cookie, whose login page is `/account/login`, and answers `login_required` to `prompt=none`; logout ends that cookie on the POST of the logout page, which carries an antiforgery token, or with a valid `id_token_hint` issued in the session of the cookie; any other request, a cross-site POST or a POST with a hint but without the cookie included, goes to the logout page, which asks, and a request without the cookie only returns to the client, with no expiring cookie (#2854). A logout ends what was issued in the session it ends ([#3198](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3198)). Every new cookie carries the id of its server session in its properties (`.fcms.server_session`), which its renewals keep as they keep the time of the sign-in; a sign-in on the login page over a live cookie of the same user keeps that id, so the browser's session goes on. The authorize endpoint issues the code of a client without a consent step under an ad-hoc authorization marked with that id (the property `fcms_server_session`), and the identity token carries it as `sid`, as RP-Initiated Logout names it. `UserSessions` revokes every valid authorization of the user marked with a session, with its tokens, whichever clients of the pool signed in through it, where the user ends the session or where no request of another site can cause it: a confirmed logout or one with the session's own hint, the member logout page, and another user's sign-in over the cookie. The provider's remote sign-out that carries the `sid` and `iss` of the sign-in revokes them too (admin SSO as built), though another site can cause it: one that knows both values can send the browser there with a top-level link, and a provider may give one `sid` to every relying party of a sign-in, so another of them knows it. Their refresh tokens are refused at once and their access tokens from the next request on, since the bearer scheme checks the token and its authorization on each request. The authorize endpoint, whose request one cross-site GET can make and which carries nothing secret, ends nothing ([#3203](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3203)): on `prompt=login`, `max_age`, `AbsoluteSessionLifetime` and a second factor that the user's role now requires and the sign-in did not give, it keeps the cookie and sends the user to the login page, and the new sign-in over the cookie carries the session on (the lifetimes below). Past `AbsoluteSessionLifetime` the session's codes and refresh tokens are refused at the token endpoint anyway, since its lifetime counts from the sign-in, and its access tokens live until they expire. So do the access tokens of a sign-in without the second factor that the role now requires, whose refresh tokens the token endpoint refuses, until the user signs in with the second factor in the same server session: that sign-in revokes the session's authorizations issued without it, with their tokens, so their access tokens are refused from the next request ([#3228](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3228)). An authorization says so in its properties (`fcms_mfa`); what the session was issued with the second factor stays, and so do the user's other sessions, whose refresh tokens are refused and whose access tokens expire. A client with `RequireConsent` has its codes issued under the user's consent, which is no session's, so its sign-in carries the server session in a claim of its own with no destination (`fcms_session`): no access or identity token names it, the identity token keeping no `sid`, and only the codes and refresh tokens, which the server alone reads, keep it. `SingleFactorTokenHandler`, on the sign-in after OpenIddict made the tokens, marks the entries of its code, access and refresh tokens without the second factor with it, and the sign-in revokes those tokens and leaves the consent. This runs at every sign-in with the second factor, whether or not the user's role requires it, so an authorization from before the mark existed, which has none, counts as one without the second factor and its session signs in once more. Only a sign-in with credentials or a code does this, so no request another site can cause revokes anything. A hint skips the logout page only when its `sid` is the cookie's session: an identity token of the same user from another session, another device or an earlier sign-in, or one without `sid`, goes to the page, which asks. Nothing is revoked where no sign-in ends: a logout that is not confirmed, a confirmation the user does not give, and a request without the cookie, a valid hint included; such a GET returns to the client without asking when its hint is an identity token signed by this server, an expired one or one a first logout revoked included, issued to the client its `client_id` names, if it names one, and whose client registered the `post_logout_redirect_uri` it asks for, if it asks for one; any other hint goes to the logout page. The user's other sessions, in another browser or on another device, stay, as does the consent of a third-party client, a permanent authorization of no server session that ends on the account page and gives no `sid`. A browser logout therefore also ends every client without a consent step that signed in through that browser, a CLI, a native app or a first-party MCP client included, whatever `offline_access` it holds; a client that must outlive a browser logout is registered with `RequireConsent`. A cookie from before sessions were marked ends no authorization, and its tokens last as before. A sign-out writes `user.sessions_ended` or `member.sessions_ended` with the reason `signed_out` after it ends the session, and one whose row cannot be written still deletes the cookie and answers as before (sessions that end as built, 4.9, #3531). `UserSessions` lists a user's valid authorizations that still hold a token in use as sessions and revokes one with its tokens, for the Management API (#2786), and the `oidc.prune` job removes unusable tokens and authorizations after 14 days. The Data Protection ring is stored unencrypted unless the host adds an encryptor, which `IDataProtectionStatus` reports, and every environment but Development refuses to start without one (`FCMS1106`, `UnencryptedKeyRingCheck`) unless `FluentCms:Security:AllowUnencryptedKeyRing` is true. A host adds it with Data Protection's own builder, `AddDataProtection().ProtectKeysWithCertificate(...)` or `ProtectKeysWithAzureKeyVault(...)` or any other `IXmlEncryptor`, which it reaches from `IServiceCollection` like any host, so Core has no pass-through for it. Export and backup packages must never include `cms_data_protection_key`, `cms_signing_key` or `cms_oidc_*`. Codes `FCMS3021` and `FCMS3022`.

**Protected resources as built** ([#2977](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2977)). A plugin's group mapped with `MapProtectedResource(prefix, options)` (`FluentCMS.Plugins.Abstractions`) is an OAuth protected resource of its own (RFC 9728), which is how the MCP plugin maps `/mcp` (5.4). The group gets what `MapGroup` gives (4.13), with four differences. Its operations enter with the origin the plugin names, `Api` or `Mcp`. It takes the Management rate limit and CORS policy, since it takes writes. No endpoint of it opts out of the signed-in caller with `AllowAnonymous()`. And a bearer token needs the scope the plugin names, one of the scopes that say which surface a token is for, which is `mcp` alone today. An API key holds no OAuth scope and is taken by its class, which the evaluator reads; a preview token carries the kind of the principal that minted it, a key's included, so it is told apart by its entry claim and refused as a token without the scope. A request without credentials is a 401 (`FCMS3102`) with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata="{metadata URL}", scope="{scope}"`, one whose credentials are refused carries `error="invalid_token"` as well, and a token without the scope is a 403 (`FCMS3103`) with `Bearer error="insufficient_scope", scope="{scope}", resource_metadata="{metadata URL}"`. Both come before the per-site gate, as on any group of a plugin. Core serves the metadata, so a plugin still maps nothing under `/.well-known` (`FCMS1524`): `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource{prefix}` answers `{ resource, authorization_servers, scopes_supported, bearer_methods_supported, resource_name }` to anyone, where `resource` is the public URL of the group, `authorization_servers` holds the issuer exactly as the discovery document names it, `scopes_supported` the scope of the resource and `bearer_methods_supported` `header`. `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource`, the document a client tries when it does not know the path of the resource, answers the same while the host has one protected resource and 404 (`FCMS3104`) once it has several, since it could describe one of them only. The documents are endpoints of the `WellKnown` group, so they take its CORS origins and count as the `connect` surface, and they are not gated per site, since an anonymous caller learns nothing of where a plugin is off. The URLs are built from the public origin and the path base (4.15), and from the request when no origin is set, as the issuer is. Under a path base the documents are under it, where RFC 9728 would put the well-known segment before it; the challenge names the URL, which is what a client follows. The authorization server takes the `resource` parameter of RFC 8707 on the authorization request and on the token request when every value is the public URL of a protected resource of the host, compared as an absolute URI with the path as written, and refuses any other with `invalid_target`; like every refusal while the authorization request is checked, it is shown on the error page rather than sent to the client. OpenIddict's own check holds the resources in a list fixed in its options, which cannot name a URL that follows the request, so it is off (`DisableResourceValidation`) and a handler of core's stands in its place. The resource permissions of clients are off too (`IgnoreResourcePermissions`), because the parameter grants nothing: the audience of a token stays `fluentcms:management`, and the scope of the resource, which a client does need a permission for, is what holds a token to it. A token with the `mcp` scope alone manages through `Origin.Mcp` only (4.8) and is refused by the Management API (403). The consent page describes `mcp` as reading and changing content as an AI agent or tool, as far as the user's roles allow. A scope that is not one of those, an origin other than `Api` or `Mcp`, and a prefix mapped as a protected resource twice stop the host with `FCMS1527`; a prefix under a group of core or with a route parameter anywhere in it is `FCMS1524`. Code `FCMS1527`.

**Dynamic client registration as built** ([#2978](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2978)). `POST /connect/register` (RFC 7591) is an endpoint of the `Connect` group, so it takes the `auth` rate limit, and core serves it only while a host has an `IMcpClientRegistrationPolicy` (`FluentCMS.Agents`, in the SDK), which the MCP plugin implements from its settings (5.4): without one, or while its `Mode` is `Off`, the endpoint is a 404 problem (`FCMS3104`) and the discovery document does not name it; otherwise the document carries `registration_endpoint`, built from the public origin as its other endpoints are.
