RFC 8414's `/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` is not served, since the protected resource metadata names the issuer and a client reads the OpenID discovery document from it. Anyone may call the endpoint, so a registration is held to what a public MCP client needs and everything else it sends is ignored and not echoed. The body is a JSON object of at most 16 KB. `redirect_uris` is 1 to 10 absolute URIs of at most 2048 characters, each `https`, or `http` on `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` or `[::1]`, with no wildcard, fragment or user information, compared exactly at the authorize endpoint, the port included (`invalid_redirect_uri`). `token_endpoint_auth_method` is `none`; RFC 7591 reads a missing one as `client_secret_basic`, and since the server gives no secret a missing one is registered as `none`, which the answer says, and any other value is refused. `grant_types` holds `authorization_code` and may hold `refresh_token`, `response_types` holds `code` alone, `scope` holds only `mcp`, `openid`, `profile`, `email` and `offline_access`, so neither `management` nor `draft`, and `client_name` is 1 to 128 characters without a control character, `MCP client` when left out. A refusal of the metadata is a 400 with the `{ error, error_description }` body of RFC 7591 (`invalid_client_metadata` unless it is about a redirect URI), not a problem document. The answer is a 201 with `client_id` (`mcp-` and 32 random hex characters), `client_id_issued_at` and the metadata as registered, with `Cache-Control: no-store` and no secret; there is no registration access token and no management of a registration by its client (RFC 7592). The client is an OpenIddict application that is public, asks the user's consent (`ConsentTypes.Explicit`), requires PKCE and holds the permissions of the code flow, of the grants it asked for and of its scopes. Its state is one property of the application, `fcms_mcp`, a JSON object with `status` (`pending`, `approved` or `rejected`), `registeredAt`, `roleId`, `approvedBy`, `approvedAt`, `usedAt` and `usedBy`, so there is no migration; a property that cannot be read, or that says approved without a role, counts as pending. With the policy `Approval` a client is pending. With `Open` it is approved at once with `DefaultRole`, a role id or the name of an instance-wide role, and is pending while that is unset or names no role, since approving it without a role would give it the user's whole reach. `MaxPendingClients` (100) caps the clients that wait: a registration beyond it is a 503 with the error `temporarily_unavailable`, `Retry-After` and log event 9005, and the count is read before the insert, so registrations that cross may pass the cap by the few in flight. `MaxClients` (1000) caps all MCP clients, counting pending, approved, rejected and those an administrator registered alike. It refuses a client's own registration in every mode, the open one included, which the pending cap does not hold back, with the same 503 and log event 9006; an administrator's registration through `IMcpClients.RegisterAsync` is counted but not refused (#3301). Both counts are one SQL query over the stored properties of the applications, and no application is loaded for them. `RegistrationsPerHour` (10) is how many clients one client address registers in an hour under either policy, counted in memory on each instance after the metadata is accepted, so a refused registration spends nothing; one beyond it is the 429 problem of the rate limits (`FCMS3110`) with `Retry-After`. A client that is not approved gets nothing. The authorize endpoint refuses it before the user is asked to sign in, on the hosted error page with `client_pending_approval` or `client_rejected`, never at its redirect URI, which is as unvetted as the client; the consent page records no consent for it, and for an approved one names the host of every redirect URI the client registered, the request's `redirect_uri` first when it is one of them, since a consent is of the user, the client and the scopes and so covers them all; each host is shown in its ASCII form (punycode for an internationalized name) with its port. It also says that the name of an MCP client, given at its registration by the client or by an administrator, is not verified (#3272). The redirect URIs of an MCP client never change after its registration; a configured client whose redirect URIs change when the host starts loses its users' consents: its permanent authorizations are revoked before the client is written, so that a start that fails between the two leaves no consent to the new URIs. A client with explicit consent has its codes issued under the consent itself, so the refresh and access tokens issued under a consent end with it; the sessions of a client without explicit consent, which has no consent to lose, stay. The URIs are compared as `Uri` values, so a host written in another case ends no consent ([#3399](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3399)); the check that a client is current compares them as they are written, since the authorize and logout endpoints compare a URI with the stored one as strings, so a redirect or post-logout URI changed only in the case of its host is written as configured at the next start and a client that sends it so is not refused ([#3442](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3442)). A redirect URI of any scheme other than `https` and `http` goes to the application that registered the scheme, whatever follows it, so the consent page names it by its scheme: `com.example.app:` for one without a host, and `myapp:` for `myapp://callback/x`, whose authority is no web host ([#3442](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3442)). A user keeps at most `MaxConsentsPerUser` (20) clients the open policy approved by consenting, and a consent that would keep one more is refused by the consent page, which ends on the hosted error page with `consent_limit_reached` (log event 9008; 5.4, "Consents and fillers"). The token endpoint, which reads the client again for a code or a refresh token, refuses a client that is not approved with `unauthorized_client`. `IMcpClients` (`FluentCMS.Agents`), which core implements over the application manager, lists the clients that registered themselves with the newest token of each as its last activity, approves a pending one with a role, rejects a pending one, removes one with its authorizations and tokens, which the store deletes with the application, so that an access token is refused from the next request on (token entry validation, above), removes those that are not approved, rejected ones included, and registered before a time, and removes those the open policy approved that registered in a span of time (5.4, "Management API"). It checks no permission, as `IJobDeadLetters` checks none: the MCP plugin's service does. A change and its event commit together (`IdentityChanges`); two changes of one client at once are told apart by the application's concurrency token, and the second is answered as not pending. A client the options registered is not an MCP client and is neither listed nor changed. Registering, approving, rejecting and removing raise `McpClientRegisteredEvent`, `McpClientApprovedEvent`, `McpClientRejectedEvent` and `McpClientRevokedEvent` (`FluentCMS.Events`), which are the activity rows `mcp.client_registered` (the anonymous actor, `Origin.Mcp`, the new client as its client, the address in `ip`, and the `status` and `role` it got), `mcp.client_approved` (with `role` and `roleName`), `mcp.client_rejected` and `mcp.client_revoked`, of no site, with the target type `mcp_client` and the detail `clientId`. The job that removes stale clients raises none. The tokens of an approved client carry its role, which the evaluator holds them to (4.8, "Client roles as built"). Log event ids 9005, 9006, 9008 and 9009; no new codes. *Registered by an administrator* ([#3102](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3102)). `IMcpClients.RegisterAsync` makes a client for an agent that does not register itself from a name, redirect URIs and a role. The name and the redirect URIs pass the checks of the endpoint, which `DynamicClientRegistration` shares; the grants and scopes are those of a registration that asks for nothing else; and the application is the same public client with PKCE and the user's consent, approved at once with the role, the caller as `approvedBy`. The policy, its `Mode` included, the cap of pending clients and the hourly count of an address belong to the anonymous endpoint and do not apply: the caller holds `mcp.manage` and is held by the Management rate limit. It raises `McpClientRegisteredEvent` with the status `approved`, the role and no address, so the row `mcp.client_registered` names the administrator as its actor. `IMcpClients.ChangeRoleAsync` gives an approved client another role and refuses a pending or rejected one, which approval answers. With the change it revokes every authorization of the client, the users' consents among them, and every token, codes and refresh tokens included, so that an access token is refused from the next request on and the next token, which comes from a new authorization request where the user consents again, carries the new role. A new role can widen what the client does, so a consent given to the old one does not carry over. Revoking the authorizations also ends what a refresh or a code redemption that read the old role just before the change goes on to issue: its tokens are issued under a revoked authorization, which token and authorization entry validation refuse at their first use. Setting the role a client has ends nothing and raises nothing. Either way a client the open policy approved then carries the caller as `approvedBy`, with the time as `approvedAt`, so that the cleanup and the bulk removal keep it; a call with no operation records the system ([#3399](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3399)). A change raises `McpClientRoleChangedEvent`, the row `mcp.client_role_changed` with `role`, `roleName` and `previousRole`, and one that crosses another change of the client is refused rather than retried. Both find a role as approval does, by its id or by the name of an instance-wide role, and neither lists nor changes a client the options registered. The state is the same property, so there is no migration.

**Sign-ins and sessions as built.** The follow-ups of the code review of 7 Nov 2026 (#2854). Lifetimes are `cms.Clients`, bound from `FluentCms:Clients`, and keep the libraries' defaults: `AccessTokenLifetime` (1 hour) and `RefreshTokenLifetime` (14 days, how long a refresh token lasts unused, since using one gives a new one) set OpenIddict's, `SignInCookieLifetime` (14 days, sliding) sets the `ExpireTimeSpan` of the Identity application cookie, and `AbsoluteSessionLifetime` (unset, no limit) is how long a session lasts after the sign-in however often it is refreshed. Each is more than zero and at most 365 days, and the access token lasts no longer than the refresh token (`FCMS3021`). `CmsSignInManager.SignInWithClaimsAsync` stores the time of the sign-in in the cookie's properties (`.fcms.signed_in_at`), which the cookie's renewals and the stamp check keep while they move its issue time. A cookie of either pool without it, or whose sign-in is past `AbsoluteSessionLifetime`, is no sign-in for the authorize endpoints and for the hosted pages that need a signed-in user (`CmsSignInManager.LiveSignIn`): the user signs in again over it, which writes the time, and the logout pages still answer. On such a cookie, on `prompt=login` and when the sign-in is older than the request's `max_age`, the authorize endpoint sends the user to the login page with the cookie left, as for a second factor (#3203); with `prompt=none` it answers `login_required`. The request the login page returns to is the same with `fcms_reauth`, a mark protected with Data Protection that says when the request asked and which sign-in it found, bound to the request's `client_id` and `code_challenge` and good for 15 minutes. It answers `prompt=login` and `max_age` only for a sign-in newer than the one found and not older than the request, by the time the sign-in wrote, so the request without one asks again. Its fields are escaped, so any client id keeps its place. A changed mark answers nothing, and so does one on a request of another client or with another `code_challenge`; on the same client and challenge it answers whatever the other parameters, such as `scope`, `state` and `nonce`. The hosted pages that need a signed-in user (change password, enable two-factor, consent, and the member pool's logins page) send the user to the login page with the cookie left when the cookie is no sign-in. The new sign-in over the cookie keeps the server session for the same user, and another user's ends it, as on the login page. What the session issued before is left as it is: its codes and refresh tokens keep the session start, security stamp and `amr` of their own sign-in, which the token endpoint checks. A request without a live cookie comes back from the login page without `login` in `prompt` and without `max_age`, which the new sign-in answers. The identity token and the access token carry `auth_time`. Codes and refresh tokens carry `fcms_session_start`, the time of the sign-in the authorize request rode on (the cookie's `.fcms.signed_in_at`, also its `auth_time`), so every authorization issued under one sign-in, of either pool, ends `AbsoluteSessionLifetime` after that sign-in however late its code flow ran; the member pool's direct login starts its own session at the grant. The token endpoint refuses a code or a refresh token past it with `invalid_grant`, so a code issued just before the lifetime ends is refused at redemption after it; a refresh token issued before the claim existed counts from its first refresh. A wrong recovery code counts towards the lockout of `cms.Users` and a locked-out user's code is refused unspent, as for the authenticator, since Identity's `TwoFactorRecoveryCodeSignInAsync` does neither. A password sign-in of a user who may not sign in, deactivated or locked out, runs the password hash before it is refused, as an unknown name does, so the time of the answer does not tell them apart. Token and authorization entry validation stay on for every bearer request: a cache keyed by token id would let a revoked token through until the cache expired, and instant revocation is worth the lookup; it can be revisited with the database work of #2857. Data Protection's hosted service is taken out by the full name of an internal type, and a unit test fails when the framework no longer has it.

**Hosted pages.** The Authorization Code flow needs server-rendered pages: login, logout, consent (third-party MCP clients), two-factor challenge and recovery codes, forgot and reset password, accept invitation, external login callback, error. They ship as Razor Pages in `FluentCMS.Core` with minimal CSS, brandable through settings (logo, colours, name) and overridable by the host. This is the one piece of UI in the backend.

**Hosted pages as built.** Razor Pages of the `FluentCms` area in `FluentCMS.Core`, which builds with the Razor SDK, at `/account/login`, `/account/logout`, `/account/consent`, `/account/two-factor`, `/account/recovery-code`, `/account/enable-two-factor`, `/account/forgot-password`, `/account/reset-password`, `/account/accept-invitation`, `/account/change-password`, `/account/external-login` and `/account/error`. `MapFluentCms` maps them with `MapRazorPages`, on the host's data source so that a host that maps its own pages gets each once, and gives the pages of the area, and only those, the `/account` conventions: the FluentCMS headers with `frame-ancestors`, the `auth` rate limit by client address (which the check of the path before authentication counts) and the `account` surface; the builder it returns passes a host's conventions on to them. No script runs on the pages: they send `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; img-src 'self' https:; base-uri 'none'` unless a page sets its own, and every form carries an antiforgery token, the logout form included, which posts to `/connect/logout` and is checked there (#2854). The antiforgery cookie is `Secure`, so a page of the area asked for over plain http is refused before it runs, in every environment: 403 and `FCMS3108`, written as the error page with a sentence that names the cause and `FluentCms:ReverseProxy:ForwardedHeaders`, where it used to fail with 500 and `FCMS3199` while it rendered or checked its form. It is not redirected, since behind a proxy whose forwarded scheme the host does not trust a redirect to https would loop; a request a known proxy forwarded as https is https by then, and a host that sets the antiforgery cookie to `SameAsRequest` is not refused ([#3159](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3159)). The page models are public (`FluentCMS.Core.Api.Account`), so a host overrides a page or the layout with its own at the same path, such as `Areas/FluentCms/Pages/Account/Login.cshtml`, on the same model. Branding is the settings area `branding` (`BrandingSettings`: `Name`, `LogoUrl`, `PrimaryColor`, `BackgroundColor`, `TextColor`), read from the instance defaults since the pages belong to no site; a colour is a hex colour and the logo an https URL or a path of the host, so neither can leave the style sheet or the markup. Login takes a user name or an email address, signs in `IdentityConstants.ApplicationScheme` with lockout, gives every refusal the same answer, and returns to a local `ReturnUrl` only, which is the authorize request the endpoint challenged from. The authorize endpoint sends a user who must change the password to `/account/change-password` (`interaction_required` for `prompt=none`), and the token endpoint redeems no code or refresh token of that user until it has; a user without a password, one of a provider only, has none to change and is not held ([#3378](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3378)). For a client with `RequireConsent`, the authorize endpoint sends a user who has not allowed it the scopes it asks to `/account/consent` (`consent_required` for `prompt=none`): consent is a permanent OpenIddict authorization that the client's later codes are issued under, so ending that session ends the consent, and refusing returns `access_denied` to the client. A logout without the logout page's token or the session's hint goes to the logout page, which asks and posts the request back; the server then returns to the client's post-logout URI or to the logout page. An error of authorize or logout that cannot go back to the client, such as an unknown client or redirect URI, goes to the error page with the server's code and description (OpenIddict's error passthrough). An exception on a page answers with a plain HTML page that names its `FCMS` code, written without the layout or the database, instead of a problem document. A reset link goes through `IEmailSender` with a token of Identity's Data Protection provider that carries the security stamp, which the reset changes, so it works once, and expires after `cms.Users.PasswordResetLifetime` (an hour); an unknown or deactivated address, or a send that fails (event 9004), gets the same answer and no email. The page only enqueues a `users.send_password_reset` job on the `mail` queue with the address and the link, whatever the address, and the job finds the user and sends, so the time of the answer does not depend on the address either (#3002). The link, like an invitation's, is built from the public origin when one is set, and otherwise from the request's scheme, host and path base, as the issuer is, which Production allows only with `AllowRequestOrigin` (public origin as built, 4.15). An invitation is accepted on `/account/accept-invitation` with the token of its link, with a password or through a provider (linking as built). The two-factor pages check an authenticator code, with `OneTimeAuthenticatorTokenProvider`, or a recovery code; enrolment and enforcement per role are in two-factor as built. The external login page signs in a user linked to the external login, creates one through a provider of `cms.Sso` (admin SSO as built), and otherwise says so. Every step of a sign-in raises `LoginSucceededEvent` or `LoginFailedEvent` from `CmsSignInManager`, so a host's own pages raise them too, with the step (`LoginMethods`), the reason of a refusal (`LoginFailureReasons`) and the client address; a refusal that matched no user keeps no name. They are the activity rows `user.login_succeeded`, whose actor is the user, and `user.login_failed`, whose actor is the anonymous principal, of no site, with the `Admin` origin and the address in `ip`. No new codes.

**Admin users.** Invitation by email (`cms_invitation` with hashed token and expiry), password policy from options, lockout, forced reset flag, deactivate, session list from OpenIddict authorizations with revoke. Password hashing uses Identity's PBKDF2 hasher behind the replaceable `IPasswordHasher<CmsUser>`; Argon2id was considered and deferred to an optional package if demand appears (Q3, which amends row 4.2 of the specification).

**Admin users as built.** `CmsUser` in `cms_user`, on Identity's EF stores for `cms_user_claim`, `cms_user_login` and `cms_user_token`. There is no `CmsRole`: a user holds roles of `cms_role` through `cms_user_role`, by id through `CmsUserManager` (instance-wide or of a site), or by name through Identity among the instance-wide roles only, since a name is unique only within its site. Roles stay with the authorization model, so there is no role store and no role claims, and no `cms_role_claim`. Password policy and lockout come from `cms.Users`, bound from `FluentCms:Users` (`FCMS3001` names a bad key), which keeps Identity's defaults with a minimum of 12 characters; an email address belongs to one user, and since sign-in takes a user name or an address, neither may be another user's other one (`CrossNameValidator`, `DuplicateUserName` or `DuplicateEmail`), at creation, at an invitation's acceptance and at a rename. No key of the database holds that pair, so a save that gives a user a name or an address runs under the `IDistributedLock` named `fluentcms:user-names` (4.15), taken before its transaction opens (`IdentityChanges.RunNamingAsync`), and `CmsUserStore` checks the pair again inside the transaction: of two saves at once, on any provider or instance, the second sees what the first committed and is refused ([#3373](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3373)). On the invitation page a name that is an email address must be the invitation's own; any other is refused with one answer, whether or not a user has it, so the page does not tell whose addresses the users have. Only a new or changed name or address is checked, so a pair that collided before the check existed keeps its other saves, its lockout among them; `CrossNameStartupCheck` logs up to 20 such pairs when the host starts (event 9007, with the two user ids) for an administrator to rename one. The lockout and the Data Protection tokens of both pools run on the host's `TimeProvider` (S8, [#3003](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3003)). In .NET 10 Identity's security stamp validator takes the container's, but nothing else used here takes one: `UserManager` reads the system clock in `IsLockedOutAsync` and `AccessFailedAsync`, and `DataProtectorTokenProvider` when it makes and checks a token. So `CmsUserManager` and `MemberManager` override those two methods with Identity's rules, a user locked out until the end of the lockout and at it, and the token providers of both pools derive from `ClockedTokenProvider`, which writes and reads Identity's token format with the time of the host's clock: a token of Identity's provider is good to it, and the other way round. A test moves the test host's clock past a lifetime or a lockout instead of waiting for it. The authenticator's codes are checked on the host's clock too, by `OneTimeAuthenticatorTokenProvider` with Identity's parameters (SHA-1, 30 second steps, two steps either way), and each is taken once: the step of the last code taken is kept with the user, as the token `AuthenticatorLastStep` of `cms_user_token`, in the save that takes it, and a code of that step or an earlier one is refused, at sign-in and at enrolment alike; of two requests with one code the second save fails on the user's concurrency stamp or, for the first code a user gives, on the key of the token row, whichever its commands meet first, and either is a refusal, since the user that failed save leaves detached is tracked again with what is stored, while another unique key the same save breaks is not that race and fails the request ([#3381](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3381)). The key is the one the provider reports, through `IDataProvider.IsUniqueViolation` with the key of the model: on PostgreSQL the constraint the error names in a field of its own; on SQL Server the key's name in single or double quotes in the message of 2601 or 2627, looked for only before the message's first parenthesis, since the duplicate value ends the message in parentheses and may repeat the name, which holds whatever the language of the server words the rest, while a message that quotes the name otherwise is thrown; and on SQLite the key's columns, which end its message, which never holds a value. The part of a message that repeats the duplicate value is never read, and a provider that cannot tell keys apart has the failure thrown ([#3447](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3447)); disabling two-factor and its reset remove the step with the key ([#3296](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3296)). A user is deactivated (`deactivated_at` and a new security stamp), never deleted: it cannot sign in (`CanSignInAsync`) and holds no roles. A password change clears `must_change_password`; a wrong current password counts towards the lockout, as at sign-in, a right one starts the count again in the save of the new password, and a user without a password (a provider's only) counts nothing. A call whose save fails puts back what it changed on the user, the flag or the deactivation, only when that save did not reload the user with what is stored, which another request may have changed since ([#3447](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3447)). Put back over a reload, the stale value would have been stored by a later save in the same scope, which could reactivate a user another request deactivated; no caller of core saves the user again after such a failure, so that was latent. The lockout itself does not refuse the change: anyone who knows a user name raises it with wrong passwords, so it holds for a new sign-in only, and a user who is signed in still changes a password it must change and still enrols an authenticator ([#3371](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3371)). What the user's own sessions get wrong is counted apart from the failed sign-ins, with the lockout's limit and time, and wrong current passwords apart from wrong codes of two-factor, as the tokens `SignedInFailures` and `SignedInCodeFailures` of `cms_user_token` (`count:lockedUntil`); a `SignedInFailures` stored before counts as wrong passwords. Either count at its limit keeps the sessions out of both for that time: the change is refused unchecked, with its own answer (`UserLockedOut`, which only the page shows), and a code gets the answer of a wrong one. A right password starts the count of passwords again, and the lockout's in its save; a right code starts the count of codes again and nothing else, since it proves the second factor and not the password, so a session that holds the second factor guesses no more passwords, and one that knows the password no more codes, than any other ([#3447](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3447)). A guess takes its place in its count before it is checked: the place is a save of the count that goes through, made again on what is stored when it loses to another save of the user on the concurrency stamp, and the place that reaches the limit keeps the sessions out from then on. Guesses a session sends at once all read the user before any of them saves, and still no more of them are checked in the lockout's time than its limit: one that finds the sessions kept out is refused with its guess unchecked, and one whose tries at a place run out, after twice the lockout's limit, which only more saves of the user going through meanwhile than the counts let pass reach, ends with the concurrency failure, its guess unchecked too. So no answer that depends on a guess comes before the save that counted it. The password change checks the new password against the policy before anything else, so that what the policy answers never depends on the current one. A wrong current password then keeps its place and counts towards the lockout, in a save of its own made again in the same way; a right one takes the new password in a save that also starts the count of passwords and the lockout's count again, its own place included, and that save is made again on what is stored, with the current password checked again, when it loses. One whose tries run out is answered as a wrong one, and its place stays counted. A code is taken in the save of its step, or of its recovery code, before anything answers it; one that loses that save is refused and counted like a wrong one, since a code is taken once, and a right one then starts the count of codes again in a save of its own. The work is not the same for every guess: one refused, or whose tries at a place run out, checks nothing; a wrong one holding a place is checked once; a right password is checked, and the new one hashed, once per try of its save, up to twice the lockout's limit. Only a guess holding a place does more, so what its time could tell is bounded by the count as its answer is. A password the operator sets with `user reset-password --password-stdin` sets the flag, since the user did not choose it, with a new stamp in the same save, and the command says so. `user create` does not set it, whether the password is typed or read from standard input: whoever runs it is usually that user, as for the first administrator of an instance, and a user made for someone else is invited, which lets the person choose the password, or flagged afterwards with `PATCH /users/{id}`. The flag is refused for a user without a password (`FCMS3049` on `mustChangePassword`), whom the page that asks for the current password would keep out ([#3378](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3378)). A renewal of the cookie, as after a password change, keeps every `amr` of the sign-in and its provider, where Identity's keeps the first only. A save of `CmsUserStore` that gives the user a new security stamp also revokes every session of the user through `UserSessions.RevokeAllAsync`, a client's consent included, in the save's transaction, so that a new stamp ends the user's access tokens at their next request as it ends its cookie, codes and refresh tokens (#3202), as the member pool does on a password change or reset. That covers a password change on `/account/change-password`, a reset, `user reset-password --password-stdin`, a new name or address set by an admin, which has its stamp in the same save ([#3226](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3226)), deactivation, removing an external login, turning two-factor on or off and its reset, and a password sign-in that rehashes a password of an older format (`CmsUserManager.CheckPasswordAsync`), whose new stamp already ended the user's other cookies and refresh tokens and now ends the user's other sessions and consents too; the flag of a forced password change gives no new stamp and revokes nothing, so the user's access tokens go on until they expire, while the token endpoint redeems none of the user's codes and refresh tokens until the password is changed. The browser that changed its password on `/account/change-password` keeps its cookie, which the page renews with the new stamp, so its clients get new tokens without a sign-in; a change through the Management API (`/me/two-factor`, `/me/logins`) ends the caller's own token with the rest. An invitation is 32 random bytes sent through `IEmailSender` and stored as SHA-256; it lasts 7 days by default (`cms.Users.InvitationLifetime`), is used once, ends when a new one goes to the same address, when the user who sent it is deactivated or when the key that sent it is revoked, and becomes the user with its roles in one transaction. Accepting it checks the sender again: a user must be active, a key neither revoked nor expired, a service account's client still configured, and each must still hold `users.manage` for the instance and every grant of the invitation's roles with the roles it has now (a service account's are those its client's configuration names), read with the management scope and without the scopes or client role of the request that sent it; otherwise the invitation is refused as not valid (`FCMS3005`), and the accept page shows it as such before the form is sent. An invitation the system sent, from the command line tool, is not checked again (#3297). Whether the sender may give those roles is checked by `InvitationService` (4.10). `IEmailSender` defaults to a sender that writes to the log in Development and refuses to send anywhere else, unless `FluentCms:Email:Smtp:Host` is set: then every environment sends through SMTP with MailKit (`EmailOptions`: `Host`, `Port` 587, `Security` `StartTls` by default, `SslOnConnect` or `None`, `UserName`, `Password` and `From`, which must parse as a mailbox; `FCMS1101` otherwise), a connection per message. User created, updated (with the names of what changed, `UserChanges`; a rehash at sign-in is none), deactivated and role changed are events staged in the outbox with the change, and `user.*` activity rows of no site. Codes `FCMS3001` to `FCMS3009`.

**The last Super Admin as built.** At least one active user always holds `*` with no constraints through an instance-wide role, which is what Super Admin holds, so that the instance can be administered without editing the database (#2850). The code review of 7 Nov 2026 asked whether this was intended or whether lockout recovery belonged to the command line tool and the system principal; it is intended (#2847, decision 5). `SuperAdminGuard` (`FluentCMS.Core.Authorization`) refuses a change that would leave no such user with `FCMS3052` (409), whoever asks, a Management API key or a service account included: deactivating a user and taking a role from it (`UserAdministration`), the roles that follow a provider's groups (admin SSO as built), and a role edit that drops the grant (4.8). The change and its events roll back, and the refused attempt is the activity row `user.change_refused` or `role.change_refused` of no site, with the `change` (`deactivate`, `remove_role`, `follow_groups` or `update`) and the `code`. Each of these changes takes the `IDistributedLock` named `fluentcms:super-admins` (4.15) before it opens a transaction, waiting up to 45 seconds. A change of a user counts the holders before and after its write inside its identity transaction, and a role edit counts, before it saves, whether anyone keeps the grant through another role. So two changes at once, on one instance or on several, run one after the other on every provider, not only on SQLite, which serialises writers anyway, and the second finds no other holder. A change that adds a holder takes no lock. An invitation can carry Super Admin (4.10), and since it only adds a holder it is unchanged. Users are not deleted (`FCMS3007`), and a role is deleted only while no user holds it (4.10), so neither can take the grant away. `CmsUserManager` does not check, so host code that calls it directly keeps a holder itself.

**Two-factor.** TOTP through Identity's authenticator support, recovery codes, enforcement per role: a role flagged `RequireTwoFactor` makes the authorize endpoint refuse a session without an `mfa` authentication method reference.

**Two-factor as built.** `TwoFactorService` (`FluentCMS.Core.Identity`) runs it on `OneTimeAuthenticatorTokenProvider` (RFC 6238 with Identity's parameters: SHA-1, 30-second steps, six digits, each code taken once, admin users as built) and Identity's store, which keeps the key and the recovery codes in `cms_user_token`, unhashed, as Identity does. A user enrols a key, which waits until a code of it turns two-factor on; setting a waiting key keeps the security stamp, so the user's sessions go on, and a new enrolment replaces it. Turning two-factor on returns ten recovery codes, shown once, and gives a new security stamp, which ends the user's sessions, its access tokens included, so the next sign-in asks for the second factor. A recovery code signs in once and is then spent. Replacing the recovery codes needs an authenticator code, and turning two-factor off needs an authenticator or a recovery code, so a stolen access token alone cannot undo the second factor; turning it off removes the key and the codes and gives a new stamp, which ends the user's sessions in the same way. A wrong code counts as a failed sign-in towards the lockout of `cms.Users` and towards the count of the user's own sessions, which is what refuses a code a signed-in user gives, at enrolment, for new recovery codes and to turn two-factor off; the lockout does not (admin users as built). Enrolling a key and turning two-factor on through `/me/two-factor/enrol` and `/me/two-factor/verify` need a token whose sign-in on the hosted pages (`auth_time`) is at most ten minutes old, since a token alone, such as one that leaked, could otherwise turn two-factor on with an authenticator of its own and keep the owner out; an older one, a refreshed one included, is refused with `FCMS3065` (403) until the user signs in again ([#3372](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3372)). The hosted page has the cookie's sign-in instead. Two-factor is on when the flag is set and a key exists, which is when Identity's sign-in asks for it; the flag alone counts as off. `cms_role.require_two_factor` is the flag, `requireTwoFactor` on the `/roles` routes (4.10). A user who holds a flagged role, instance-wide or of a site, needs the authentication method reference `mfa` on the Identity cookie, which Identity writes after the authenticator or a recovery code, and `pwd` after a password alone. Without it, the authorize endpoint sends a user who has two-factor to the login page again with the cookie left (`login_required` for `prompt=none`), and sends one who has none to `/account/enable-two-factor` (`interaction_required` for `prompt=none`). That page enrols a key, turns two-factor on with a code of it, shows the recovery codes once and signs the cookie in again with `mfa` beside the methods it had (`pwd`, or the provider), since the user has just given both factors. The check comes before the forced password change, and the token endpoint refuses a code or refresh token without `mfa` of such a user (`invalid_grant`), so a session from before the flag ends at its next refresh. **Remembered devices.** The two-factor page's "Do not ask again on this device" (Identity's `TwoFactorRememberMe` cookie) skips the second factor and signs in with `pwd`, so a remembered device does not satisfy `RequireTwoFactor`: `CmsSignInManager.IsTwoFactorClientRememberedAsync` answers false for a user who holds a flagged role, who is therefore asked for the second factor at every sign-in, and the page does not offer that user the option. `amr` stays accurate this way. An external sign-in carries no `amr`, so a flagged role asks for the local second factor after it too, unless its provider is trusted for its own `mfa` (admin SSO as built). Identity's stamp check rebuilds the cookie's principal every half hour, and keeps `amr` through `SecurityStampValidatorOptions.OnRefreshingPrincipal`. The authorize endpoint copies `amr` from the cookie into the code, and the code and refresh tokens carry it into the access and identity tokens. **Resetting another user's two-factor.** A user who lost the app and every recovery code, or whose role requires two-factor and who cannot finish enrolling, is reset by an administrator with `POST /users/{id}/two-factor/reset` (4.10, #2851). `UserAdministration.ResetTwoFactorAsync` needs `users.manage` for the instance and every grant of the user's roles, as any change of a user does (`FCMS3103`), and a caller that is a user whose access token carries the `amr` `mfa` (`FCMS3064`, 403). A Management API key or a service account has no second factor and is refused, and so is a user signed in with a password alone. The request principal carries the token's `amr` values as the claim `amr` for this check. A user does not reset its own two-factor (`FCMS3049`): it turns it off with a code through `/me/two-factor/disable`. A user with neither the flag nor a key has nothing to reset (`FCMS3061`). The reset removes the key, the recovery codes and the flag with a new security stamp in one save, which ends the user's codes, refresh tokens and cookie and revokes every session of the user through `UserSessions`, so its access tokens stop at once too. A role that requires two-factor then sends the user to `/account/enable-two-factor` at its next sign-in. The reset raises `UserTwoFactorResetEvent`, staged in the outbox with the change, which is the activity row `user.two_factor_reset` of no site with the administrator as its actor; the store's `user.updated` row names `twoFactor` too when the flag was on. Codes `FCMS3060` to `FCMS3065`.

**Admin SSO.** External OIDC providers (Entra ID, Okta, Keycloak and generic OIDC are the same handler with presets) configured in the host configuration and shown read-only in the admin in the first release (runtime editing is a Should). Federation happens on the hosted login page: auto-provisioning, default role, group claim to role mapping, optional local-login disable.

**Admin SSO as built.** `cms.Sso`, bound from `FluentCms:Sso` (`FCMS3070` names a bad key), lists the providers under `Providers:{name}`. A name is 1 to 64 letters, digits, `-` or `_`, and is the provider's authentication scheme, its login provider in `cms_user_login` and its callback path, `/account/sso/{name}`. A name is found whatever its case: `CmsUserStore` finds a login of a provider under the name as it is written and, failing that, under another case of it (other schemes match as written), so a change of case in configuration keeps the users linked ([#3010](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3010)), and a name that is another authentication scheme's in any case, added before the providers or after them, stops the host (`FCMS3070`). Every provider is ASP.NET Core's OpenID Connect handler, added to the authentication options when the host starts. `Preset` (`Oidc`, `EntraId`, `Okta` or `Keycloak`) only builds the authority and names the button: from `TenantId` (`https://login.microsoftonline.com/{tenant}/v2.0`; `common`, `organizations` and `consumers` are refused, since their tokens come from any tenant, and so are the consumers tenant's id `9188040d-6c67-4c5b-b112-36a304b66dad` and an `Authority` set by hand whose first path segment is one of these on a Microsoft sign-in host of any cloud, a trailing dot ignored; the refusal is unconditional, since there is no list of allowed tenants, #3301), from `Domain` and `AuthorizationServerId` (`https://{domain}`, or `https://{domain}/oauth2/{id}`), or from `BaseUrl` and `Realm` (`{base}/realms/{realm}`). `Authority` overrides it, and generic OIDC requires it. The handler runs the code flow with PKCE, with `ClientSecret` when one is set, asks for `openid profile email` and the `Scopes` listed, takes the answer as a form post, and signs into Identity's external cookie with the token's claims under their own names; a host tunes a provider with `services.Configure<OpenIdConnectOptions>(name, ...)`. The login page offers a button per provider, and the external login page asks `SsoSignIn`. A user linked to the login signs in. Someone with no user gets one when `AutoProvision` is on (the default), named and addressed by the `EmailClaim` (`email`), with no password, linked in `cms_user_login` and holding `DefaultRole` and the mapped roles, in one transaction, so the user created and role changed events are staged with it (event 9040). A role is named by its id or by the exact name of an instance-wide role; one that does not exist is not given (`FCMS3071`, event 9042). `RoleMappings` is a list of `Group` and `Role`, matched exactly against the values of `GroupsClaim` (`groups`). No group gives a role by its name, so Super Admin is given only by a mapping or a default role that names it. At every later sign-in of a user who is neither deactivated nor locked out, the mapped roles follow the token's groups: a mapped role whose group is gone is taken away, and the default role and the roles the provider does not map stay. A token with no groups claim at all takes the mapped roles away too, and logs event 9044, since Entra ID's group overage or a missing scope looks the same. Groups that would take Super Admin from its last active holder change no role and refuse the sign-in (`not_allowed`, event 9045, `user.change_refused` with `follow_groups`), since the provider no longer vouches for that user and the instance keeps its last Super Admin (admin users as built). A token without an email address, or that does not say `email_verified` is true while the provider's `RequireVerifiedEmail` is on (the default; off, only a false one is refused) (`FCMS3072`), or whose address belongs to a user not linked to the provider (`FCMS3073`), creates no user, and a creation that fails (`FCMS3074`, with Identity's codes only) leaves none, so an address is never taken over unless the provider links existing users (linking as built). Those refusals log event 9043 and raise `LoginFailedEvent` (`external`, `not_allowed`, no user). The provider's remote sign-out path, `/account/sso/{name}/signout`, is its front-channel logout ([#3204](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3204)). A sign-in through a provider keeps the provider's name and the `sid` and `iss` of its validated id token in the sign-in cookie's properties (`.fcms.sso_provider`, `.fcms.sso_sid`, `.fcms.sso_iss`), carried over from the external cookie only when that cookie's subject is the user's own login of the provider, also when the local second factor follows the provider, and kept by the cookie's renewals; a second factor given after another sign-in at the provider reached the callback in the same browser keeps none, so no remote sign-out ends that sign-in. A request to the path ends something only when it carries both `sid` and `iss`, each once, in the query of a GET or the form of a POST, both equal to the cookie's, compared exactly, and the sign-in was made through that provider: it then ends the server session's authorizations with the Identity application cookie, as a confirmed logout does (the authorization server as built). Each answer is the same empty 200 with `Cache-Control: no-cache, no-store`; a sign-out logs event 9046 and a request that ends nothing event 9047, without the values it carried. Anything else ends nothing: a request in which either occurs more than once, a request without them or with other values, a sign-in whose token had no `sid`, and a sign-in made another way, with a password or through another provider, whatever the `sid`. The `OnRemoteSignOut` event makes the check and always ends the request, since the handler's own check compares `sid` and `iss` only when the cookie's principal holds them as claims and is skipped otherwise. A host's own events do not drop it ([#3225](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3225)): after every `Configure`, the provider's events become FluentCMS's (`ChainedOpenIdConnectEvents`, which the member providers share, #3526), whose methods `TokenValidated`, `RemoteFailure`, `RemoteSignOut` and `RedirectToIdentityProvider` (the callback on the public origin) run FluentCMS's part first and then the event's delegate, which calls the host's events, whether the host replaced `Events` or set `EventsType` (resolved once a request, as the handler does); every other event is the host's alone. A delegate a host sets later, as `options.Events.OnRemoteSignOut` in a `PostConfigure` registered after `AddFluentCms`, replaces only the host's part. A host that replaces `Events` or sets `EventsType` in such a `PostConfigure` would drop the events altogether, so it does not start (`FCMS3070`, naming the provider), since the events are read once every `PostConfigure` has run, when the host starts. They are read apart from the authentication options: `AddOpenIdConnect`, which a host may call for a scheme of its own, as Microsoft.Identity.Web does, registers a post-configuration that reads the authentication options while it builds a scheme's options, so read while those are built they would wait on themselves ([#3531](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3531)). A sign-out writes `user.sessions_ended` with the reason `remote_sign_out` and the system as its actor (4.9). A provider is therefore set up for front-channel logout with the session required (`frontchannel_logout_session_required`, so that it sends `sid` and `iss`), with `sid` in its id tokens and the path as its front-channel logout URI. The provider loads that URI in an iframe on its own page (OpenID Connect Front-Channel Logout 1.0), and a request made in a cross-site iframe does not carry the `SameSite=Lax` sign-in cookie, so it ends nothing: the path ends a sign-in only when the request carries the cookie, which a page of another site sends only with a top-level GET navigation, never with a POST or in an iframe, and a page on the same site as this host, such as a provider on a sibling subdomain, sends with any request; otherwise the session lasts until the user logs out here or the cookie and the tokens expire. Back-Channel Logout, which needs no cookie, is not built, since it needs the server to find sessions by the provider's `sid`, a change of the model. A provider's error, or a callback that belongs to no sign-in started here, goes back to the login page as a failed sign-in (`invalid_credentials`, event 9041) rather than to an error page. **The provider's `amr`.** It counts only from a provider with `TrustMfaClaim`, which is off by default: a token of such a provider whose `amr` holds `mfa` signs in with `amr=mfa` and without the local second factor. Any other external sign-in carries no `amr`, so a role with `RequireTwoFactor` asks for the local second factor after the provider, or sends the user to `/account/enable-two-factor`, as two-factor as built says. `DisableLocalLogin`, which needs a provider, hides the password form and the forgotten-password link, and `CmsSignInManager` refuses every password sign-in (`not_allowed`). A user created by a provider has no password; with local login on, a reset link gives it one. With local login off, an invitation is accepted through a provider (linking as built). Providers come from configuration only: runtime editing and a read-only list in the admin are not built. Codes `FCMS3070` to `FCMS3074`.

**Linking external logins as built.** The page that accepts an invitation offers a button per provider of `cms.Sso` next to the password form, and with `DisableLocalLogin` the providers only, since `InvitationService` then refuses a password (`FCMS3093`). The provider comes back to that page, and `SsoSignIn` accepts the invitation with the external login when the address of the provider's `EmailClaim` is the invitation's, as Identity normalizes addresses, and the token does not say `email_verified` is false (`FCMS3072`); another address is refused (`FCMS3092`) and the invitation stays pending. The user is named by the invitation's address, has no password, holds the invitation's roles and is linked in the transaction that uses the invitation up; it then signs in as at any sign-in through the provider, so the mapped roles follow its groups and `TrustMfaClaim` and `RequireTwoFactor` apply as before, and the default role is not given. An error or a cancel at the provider returns to the invitation. `LinkExistingUsers` on a provider, off by default, links at its first sign-in through the provider the user whose address the token carries and who has no login of that provider, only when the token says `email_verified` is true, so a provider that sends no such claim links no one; a user who cannot sign in now is not linked, and without the option or the claim the address is refused as before. An invitation needs no explicit `email_verified` because its link already showed that the person reads the mailbox, and an existing user has no such proof. Linking and removing a login raise `UserLoginLinkedEvent` and `UserLoginUnlinkedEvent` from `CmsUserStore` with the change, the activity rows `user.login_linked` and `user.login_unlinked` with the provider. `GET /users/{id}/logins` (`users.manage`) and `GET /me/logins` list `{ "provider", "providerKey", "displayName" }`, and `DELETE /users/{id}/logins/{provider}` (with every grant of the user's roles too) and `DELETE /me/logins/{provider}` remove the user's logins of that provider (`FCMS3091` when it has none) with a new security stamp, which ends the user's sessions, its access tokens included. The last way to sign in is not removed (`FCMS3090`, 409): a login counts while its provider is offered on the login page, and a password while local login is on. With a public origin (4.15), the `redirect_uri` a provider is given, and the code is redeemed with, is built from it in the provider's `RedirectToIdentityProvider`, before a delegate the host set. Codes `FCMS3090` to `FCMS3093`; event ids 9060 to 9062.

**API keys.** `cms_api_key (id, name, class, key_prefix, key_hash, role_id, site_ids json, type_names json, expires_at, last_used_at, rate_limit, revoked_at, created_by)`. Classes: `delivery` (read published), `preview` (read drafts too), `management` (whatever the bound role allows). Format `fcms_dk_...`, `fcms_pk_...`, `fcms_mk_...` with 32 random bytes, shown once, stored as SHA-256. Sent as `Authorization: ApiKey <key>` or `X-Api-Key`. A dedicated authentication handler produces a principal with the role's claims and the key class as scope.

**API keys as built.** `cms_api_key` also has `created_at`. A key is stored with its SHA-256 and its first 16 characters, which find the row; the hash is compared in constant time. The key is returned once, by the create call, and no event, activity row or log holds it or its hash. A key is bound to one role, and to sites and content types when the lists are given; an empty list is refused (`FCMS3020`), since a key must not read it as every site. A role is not deleted while a key that is not revoked uses it (4.10). The `ApiKey` scheme reads `Authorization: ApiKey <key>` or `X-Api-Key` and puts these claims on the request: `sub` (key id), `name`, `fcms_kind` (`ApiKey`), `roles` (the role id), `scope` (the class), and `fcms_sites`, `fcms_types` and `fcms_rate_limit` when the key has them. A wrong, expired or revoked key fails authentication with `FCMS3016` alike, so the answer does not tell that a secret was ever a key, and so does a request that carries a key and any other credential, two keys included (`FCMS3019`). The reason stays on the server (#2854): each refusal logs event 9010 (`ApiKeyRefused`, Information) with its code (`FCMS3016`, `FCMS3017` expired, `FCMS3018` revoked or `FCMS3019`), the client address and the id of a key that was found, never the key, and adds one to the counter `fluentcms.api_keys.refused` by `fluentcms.code`. It writes no activity row: a database write per refused key would let a flood of keys write as fast as it is sent, and the rate limit before authentication already bounds it. The `FluentCms` scheme, which the FluentCMS endpoints authenticate with, takes a request with a key to the `ApiKey` scheme and any other to the bearer scheme the authorization server registers, and leaves it anonymous when a host has none. It never falls back to the host's default scheme, which may be a cookie and would open the APIs to cross-site requests, and a scheme that forwards back to it gets no result instead of recursing. `last_used_at` is written at most once every five minutes. The binding is enforced by the evaluator, not only over HTTP: a principal with `fcms_sites` reaches only operations in those sites, so it writes no global type, and one with `fcms_types` holds only the content permissions of those types; a claim that names nothing usable reaches nothing. The evaluator reads a key's role from `cms_api_key` on each request, so a revoked or expired key holds no role at once. A schedule made by a key checks the key as it is when the job runs, its whole binding read again, so a revoked, expired, deleted or rebound key publishes nothing and the schedule ends `failed`. Expiry can be brought forward, never extended, by a conditional update. Key created, key revoked and key expiry changed are events, staged in the outbox with the change, and `api_key.*` activity rows of no site. `SecretScrubber` masks an `ApiKey` credential and anything shaped like a key. Codes `FCMS3010` to `FCMS3020`.

**API key addresses and rotation as built** ([#2936](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2936)). `cms_api_key` also has `allowed_addresses json`, `previous_key_prefix`, `previous_key_hash` and `previous_key_expires_at`, indexed on `previous_key_prefix`. *Allowed addresses.* A key may name up to 64 IP addresses and CIDR ranges it is accepted from (`allowedAddresses`); left out or null it is accepted from any address, and an empty list is refused (`FCMS3020`) as for sites and types. An entry is an IPv4 address in its dotted form of four numbers, an IPv6 address, or either with a prefix (`203.0.113.0/24`, `2001:db8::/32`); a range with bits set past its prefix, an IPv4-mapped IPv6 entry, a zone and anything else are refused with `FCMS3024`, and the entries are stored in their plain form without duplicates. The handler checks the client address the host trusts, `Connection.RemoteIpAddress` after the forwarded headers as `FluentCms:ReverseProxy` configures them, once the key is found valid and active; an IPv4 client seen as IPv4-mapped IPv6 is matched as IPv4, and a request with no known address is refused. A refused address fails with `FCMS3016` like a wrong key, so a stolen key does not tell that it would work from elsewhere, and the log has `FCMS3023` (event 9010) with the address and the key's id. Since only a holder of a valid key reaches this check, it is the one refusal that writes an activity row: `ApiKeyAddressRefusedEvent`, the row `api_key.address_refused` of no site with the anonymous actor, the address in `ip` and the detail `code`, at most once per key and address and ten addresses per key every five minutes on each instance; a row that cannot be written is logged (event 9011) and the request is still a 401. `PUT /api-keys/{id}/allowed-addresses` replaces the list (null for any address); it needs what creating the key would, its role's grants included, since widening where a key is accepted is a decision like a new key's, and raises `ApiKeyAllowedAddressesChangedEvent` (`api_key.allowed_addresses_changed`, with the list). A job a key scheduled has no client address and is not held to the list. *Rotation.* `POST /api-keys/{id}/rotate` gives the key a new secret of its class and keeps its id, role, binding, expiry and list; the answer is the key with its `secret`, as on create, and no other answer has it. The secret it replaces moves to `previous_key_*` and is still accepted until `previous_key_expires_at`, `cms.ApiKeys.RotationOverlap` ahead (`FluentCms:ApiKeys:RotationOverlap`, 24 hours by default as for webhook secrets, from zero to 30 days, `FCMS3001` otherwise), or `{ "overlapSeconds" }` ahead when the caller asks for a shorter one; zero refuses it at once, and a longer overlap than the option's is refused with `FCMS3026`. A second rotation within the overlap replaces the previous secret with the one it replaces, so a key has at most two. The previous secret is found by its own prefix and compared in constant time like the current one, and once its overlap has passed it is a wrong key (`FCMS3016`); revoking or expiring the key refuses both. A revoked or expired key is not rotated (`FCMS3025`, 409), and the update is conditional on the secret that was read, so of two rotations at once the second gets `FCMS3025` instead of returning a secret that never worked. While the previous secret is accepted, the answers carry `previousKeyExpiresAt`. Every answer that carries a key also has `maxOverlapSeconds`, the longest overlap a rotation takes: `RotationOverlap` in whole seconds, rounded down, so a fractional option is answered as the largest whole number of seconds a rotation may ask for, and what a rotation that leaves `overlapSeconds` out gets, so that a client can offer the range before it asks ([#3497](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3497)); it is the same for every key, revoked and expired ones included, and only a caller who may read the key reads it, while the `FCMS3026` refusal already names it. Rotating raises `ApiKeyRotatedEvent` (`api_key.rotated`, with `overlapEndsAt`), which, like the activity row and the log, holds neither secret nor its hash. Codes `FCMS3023` to `FCMS3026`.

**Preview tokens as built** ([#2934](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2934)). `POST /api/management/v1/content/{type}/{id}/preview-token` mints a short-lived token that reads the draft of that one entry on the Delivery API, so a front end that renders drafts holds no preview key. The body is optional: `{ "minutes", "locale" }`. `minutes` is from 1 to `FluentCms:PreviewTokens:MaximumLifetime` (one hour by default, at most a day) and `DefaultLifetime` (ten minutes) when left out, both whole minutes; another value is refused with `FCMS3027` (422), and options out of range stop the host with `FCMS3001`. `locale` binds the token to that one locale, and a type that is not localized takes none (`FCMS2108`). Minting needs the `management` scope and reads the draft first as the caller, in the named locale with no fallback when there is one, so a caller who may not read that draft, by its roles, a row rule or a binding, gets the 404 of the read and no token. The answer is `{ "token", "scheme": "Preview", "expiresAt", "expiresIn", "type", "id", "site", "locale", "url", "publishedUrl" }` with `Cache-Control: no-store`. `expiresIn` is the lifetime in whole seconds, a JSON integer, and `expiresAt` less the token's `iat`, so a client whose clock is not the server's, or that cannot read the `Date` header across origins, ends the token that long after its own request ([#3473](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3473)). The token is a JWT signed with the authorization server's signing key (RS256), with the audience `fluentcms:preview` and the header `typ` `fcms-preview+jwt`, so an access token is never read as one and it is never read as an access token. It carries the caller's `sub`, `name` and `fcms_kind`, the scope `preview` alone, `fcms_sites` (the request's site), `fcms_types` (the type), `fcms_entry` and, when bound, `fcms_locale`, and a service account's `roles` and a user's `amr` as the caller's token had them, so a role that requires two-factor still holds. It is sent as `Authorization: Preview <token>`, which the `FluentCms` scheme gives to the `Preview` handler; with any other credential it is refused (`FCMS3019`), a token that does not validate is `FCMS3029` and one past its `exp` is `FCMS3030`, both 401, checked against the `TimeProvider` with no clock skew. The principal is the caller with those claims: an admin user's and an API key's roles are read again on every request, so deactivating the user, revoking the key or taking the role away stops the token at once. The engine holds it to its binding whatever reads it: the site and type bindings of API keys, and the entry, which `EffectivePermissions` adds to the row rule as `id = {entry}`, so no read on any surface, a plugin's included, reaches another entry directly. *Targets* ([#3095](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3095)). The targets of the entry's relations, at every level of `populate`, and those of the entry links of its rich text, are read in their published rows alone, as the principal that minted the token reads published content at the time of the request: with its grants and row rules, read again on every request, so a grant it loses stops populating from the next request; in the token's site, so a target of another site is out; with no scope, so the draft of a target is never read and a target that was never published is left out; and without the binding to the entry and its type. A target that principal may not read is left out as a Management read leaves it out: null for a single relation, gone from a multiple one, and an entry link loses its mark and keeps its text. A token minted by an API key stays held to the types the key is bound to, which are read again with the key's role rather than carried in the token, whose payload the browser that holds it can read. The depth and entry caps of population (4.4) count the targets with the entry, and a path through a type that principal may not read is refused as it is for that principal. A read by id takes no filter or sort, and in a list a filter or sort through a relation stays under the token's own access, which reads no target. The locale is held by Delivery alone, since the values a locale falls back to are read in other locales. Without the `management` scope it writes nothing and mints nothing (403, 4.10). *Stateless.* Nothing is stored and there is no migration; a token cannot be revoked by itself, and signing out does not end it. Its lifetime cap bounds that, and the role and deactivation checks end it for a caller who loses access. A token store with revocation was left out, as a table for a credential that lasts minutes. A token minted by an API key with allowed addresses is not held to them, since it is meant for a browser elsewhere; it reads one draft for at most the cap. *URL* ([#3099](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/3099)). The core settings area `preview` (`PreviewSettings`, `settings.preview.manage`), per site with instance defaults, holds `templates`: an absolute `http` or `https` URL template per content type, by the type's name, such as `https://www.example.com/{locale}/articles/{slug}?preview={token}`. The placeholders are `{id}`, `{locale}` (a localized type's only), `{token}` and the name of a field of the type that holds one value: a text, slug, select, email, URL, integer, decimal, Boolean, date or date and time field that is neither hidden nor multiple. A placeholder stands in the path, the query or the fragment, never in the scheme, the host or the port; `{token}` is there once, as the whole value of one query parameter; a `%` of the template is a whole escape such as `%20`, so that no value can finish one (`.%{slug}` with `2e` would read as a step up the path); the path does not start with `//`, which a front end that sends the path on as a relative redirect would read as another host; and a template has no user name, at most 2,048 characters and only the characters of a URL. A write or an import of the area refuses a template that breaks these rules (`FCMS3031`) and one for a type the schema does not have or with a placeholder the type cannot fill (`FCMS3032`), on the path `templates.{type}`. The answer's `url` is the type's template in the site filled with the draft's values and the new token, and `publishedUrl` is the same template, without the token's parameter, filled with the values of the published row of the locale that gave the draft, read with no fallback, or null when the entry has no published row in that locale; both are null when the type has no template. Every value is escaped as URL data, so it adds no segment, parameter or fragment, and the token goes only where `{token}` is. `{locale}` is the site's code (`en`, never the `EN` a request may send) of the locale whose row gave the draft: the token's locale, or for a token of none the first locale of the request's chain in which the entry has a row, as a read by id is served, so an entry with English rows alone read in French has the English pages. The values are those the caller's own reads show, which are those the token's read shows, so a field the caller may not read leaves the URL null rather than putting its value in it, and so does a field that is empty, that is not one value or that holds only dots, and a template the schema no longer fits, such as one that names a field a later version of the code dropped. A caller who may mint a token gets the URL without reading the area, which needs `settings.preview.manage` as every area does. `publishedUrl` serves the admin's toggle between the draft and the published page; a front end whose two pages differ by more than the token's parameter, such as one with a preview route of its own, would need a second template, which is left for later. Codes `FCMS3027` to `FCMS3032`.

**Members.** A separate pool (decision 33): `cms_member*` tables with their own `UserManager<CmsMember>`, `cms_member_group` and membership table. REST endpoints under `/api/members/v1`: `register`, `verify-email`, `resend-verification`, `forgot-password`, `reset-password`, `me`. Tokens come from OpenIddict with the members audience; login runs through the hosted pages (code with PKCE) and, when `Members:AllowDirectLogin` is on, through a custom `member_password` grant for first-party apps (Q4). External providers for members: Google and Microsoft (in-box handlers), GitHub and Apple (Q10), generic OIDC (member providers as built). Member groups appear in row rules and public access.

**Members as built** ([#2992](https://github.com/fluentcms/FluentCMS/issues/2992)). `CmsMember` in `cms_member (id, email, normalized_email, email_confirmed, password_hash, security_stamp, concurrency_stamp, display_name, lockout_end, access_failed_count, deactivated_at, created_at, last_login_at)`, on Identity's EF store for `cms_member_login` and `cms_member_token`, in the system stream. The pool is one for the instance: a member belongs to no site, an address is one member, unique by its normalized form, and the address is the sign-in name. `CmsMember` derives from Identity's user, and what a member has not is fixed in the class and has no column: no user name of its own, no phone number, no second factor, no claims, and lockout that is always on. `MemberManager` (the pool's `UserManager<CmsMember>`) and `MemberSignInManager` run on `MemberIdentityOptions`, made from `cms.Members`, bound from `FluentCms:Members` (`FCMS3160` names a bad key), and not on the `IdentityOptions` of the container, which are the admin users': the pool has its own password policy (Identity's, with a minimum of 12 characters), its own lockout, sign-in with a verified address only, and token providers of its own. The hasher is `IPasswordHasher<CmsMember>`, PBKDF2 as for the admin users (Q3) and replaceable the same way. A member is deactivated (`deactivated_at` and a new security stamp) and, unlike an admin user, can be deleted: its logins, tokens and group memberships go with it, and activity rows keep its id. Nothing of the pool is the admin pool's: the managers, the token providers, the sign-in cookie and the pages are types, purposes and schemes of their own.

*Registration and mail.* `MemberAccountService` is behind `/api/members/v1` (4.10) and the member pages alike. `AllowRegistration` is false by default, and registering then answers 404 (`FCMS3104`) on the route and on the page. Registering, asking for the verification mail again and asking for a reset return nothing whatever the address, so the answer never tells whether it is a member's; what is wrong with the request itself, an address that is none or a password the policy refuses, is told to anyone, since it says nothing of who is a member. A registration of an unknown address makes the member, not verified, and sends the verification mail. One of an address that is registered with a password and not verified sets no password, because nobody has shown yet that the address is theirs: keeping the first password would let whoever registered first sign in once the owner verifies, and taking the new one would let whoever registered last, by registering between the owner's registration and the owner's click. So the password of neither is kept. The member loses its password, gets one new security stamp, which ends the links sent so far, leaves every group it was put in, and counts as made now, so that the job below leaves the new link its time; the mail carries the link that sets a password, which is the reset link and verifies the address too, so the owner of the mailbox is the one who chooses the password. This happens once: every later registration of the address finds a member without a password and changes nothing, so the stamp stops there and nobody can keep ending the owner's links. A member that a provider made without saying its address is verified (member providers as built) is such a first registration too: it has an external login in place of a password, which goes the same way when the address is registered, with the same mail. A sign-in through a provider with such an address, by a login that is not the member's, is a second claim like a registration and does the same (`ContestAsync`). One of a verified or a deactivated address, or of a member without a password, which is also what a member an administrator made is until it uses its link, changes nothing and sends a mail that says the address has an account; a registration never gives such a member a password, which only a link sent to its mailbox does. Two registrations of a pending address at once end as one, and both are answered alike. The password is hashed in each case, and a mail goes out in each, so the time of the answer tells little. `resend-verification` and `forgot-password` only check that the address is one and enqueue a `members.send_mail` job on the `mail` queue (4.12), whatever the address, as the admin pool's reset does; the job finds the active member, makes the token and sends, and sends nothing to an address that is no member's. So the request does the same work for every address, and the time of the answer tells nothing of it (#3002). The job's payload holds the address; the Management API never shows a payload, and the job retention removes it with a job that succeeded, and with a dead-lettered one after `JobRunnerOptions.DeadLetteredRetention` (30 days). Every address of the right form enqueues a job, so requests from many client addresses, each within its `auth` limit, can make the queue wait; a job of an unknown address is one lookup. The tokens are Identity's Data Protection tokens with purposes of the pool (`FluentCmsMemberEmail`, `FluentCmsMemberPasswordReset`), so a token of the admin pool is never a member's, and they carry the security stamp: `EmailVerificationLifetime` (24 hours) and `PasswordResetLifetime` (1 hour), on the host's `TimeProvider` as the lockout (Admin users as built). Verifying gives a new stamp, where Identity's own confirmation keeps it, and a reset changes it, so each token works once; a wrong, used or expired token and an unknown address are answered alike (`FCMS3166`). A reset verifies the address too, since its link reached the mailbox, which is how a member an administrator created, who has no password, gets both. A reset of an address that was not verified also removes the member's external logins, in the same change, since they date from before anyone showed whose address it is. A reset and a change of password end every session of the member, access tokens included, and a wrong current password on `change-password` counts towards the lockout, as for admin users: a locked-out member gets the answer a wrong password gets (`FCMS3167`, unchanged), a right one starts the count again in the save of the new password, and a member without a password, one of a provider only, counts nothing, so its own calls cannot lock its provider's sign-in out. `MemberMail` sends through `IEmailSender`. A link goes to the front end when `Links:VerifyEmail` or `Links:ResetPassword` is set, an absolute `http` or `https` URL with `{email}` and `{token}` after its host (`FCMS3160` otherwise), and to the member page on the public origin (4.15) when it is not; a link is never built from a request, so with neither no mail goes out, the log has event 9071 with `FCMS3173`, and the answer is as before. An address gets one mail a minute of each of two slots (`MemberMailLimiter`): the mail its owner asks for, a reset or the verification mail again, and the link an administrator sends a new member, take one; what a registration or a sign-in through a provider sends, the verification mail of a new member, the note that the address has an account and the link to choose a password, takes the other, since anyone who knows the address can cause it. So a visitor who keeps registering the owner's address never holds back the reset the owner asks for, and the address gets at most two mails a minute. A Requested mail needs only the address too, so a visitor can hold back the owner's next request for a minute, but what goes out is still a working link to the owner's mailbox. The time of the last mail of a slot is kept in `IDistributedCache` under a SHA-256 hash of the normalized address, never the address, for the minute, and with a shared cache it is read and written under the distributed lock `members.mail_limit`, so of two instances that ask at once one sends. Every instance sees the limit once the host registers a shared cache; the default `MemoryDistributedCache` keeps it per instance, as it keeps everything (4.13), and the instance then decides it alone, without the lock, so N instances send an address up to N mails a minute of each slot; the startup report says so outside Development (`member-mail-limit-per-instance`, 4.15). A mail held back, also when the lock is not free within 10 seconds, is event 9072, and a send that failed, or a limit that could not be read, 9070; neither changes the answer. The recurring job `members.remove_unverified` removes, once a day, the members that have not verified their address `UnverifiedMemberLifetime` (7 days) after they were made, a member an administrator created included; such a member never signed in, and the job raises no event (9073). It counts from when the member was made, not from its last mail, and a link to a member that is not verified ends when the job may remove the member: the mail gives the earlier of the token's lifetime and `created_at` plus `UnverifiedMemberLifetime` as its expiry, and both token providers refuse the token after it. So a link asked for on the last day is good for the hours it says, and once none are left no mail goes (event 9076). A member with a password or a provider login that is registered again then counts as made now and gets a new link; one with neither, such as a member an administrator made, waits until the job removes it, at most a day, and is registered afresh. Keeping the time of the last mail instead would need a column the table does not have (#3002).
